is large, the system state can not overlap much with
\phi_{2}
.
The problem of tunneling appears when
|\mu_{2}-\mu_{1}|

is exponentially small for the order
e^{S_{0}/h}
.
Thus, we may only consider
W_{\alpha\beta}
for those
\alpha,\penalty\ \beta
satisfying the following assumption:

Assumption A.5
.

\mu_{\alpha}-\mu_{\beta}=O(h^{\infty})e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}},

(215)

\phi_{\alpha}=O(h^{-N_{0}}e^{d(x,U_{j(\alpha)})/h}),

(216)

where
N_{0}\in\mathbb{N}
is a constant.

A sufficient condition to get (
216
) is that

U_{j(\alpha)}
is a point and

\displaystyle\min f=f(U_{j(\alpha)})=0,\penalty\ \nabla f(U_{j(\alpha)})=0,\penalty\ \nabla^{2}f(U_{j(\alpha)})\geq 0,

(217)

\displaystyle\mu_{\alpha}\in[0,C_{0}h],\penalty\ \mathrm{for\penalty\ some}\penalty\ C_{0}>0.

(218)

Under
Assumption
A.5
, first note that

W_{\alpha\beta}=O(h^{\infty})e^{-S_{0}/h},\penalty\ \mathrm{if}\penalty\ j(\alpha)=j(\beta)\vee d(U_{j(\alpha)},U_{j(\beta)})>S_{0},

(219)

which is negligible as the principal terms are of the order
h^{-\nu}e^{-S_{0}/h}
for some
\mu>0
.
The remained case to consider is

j(\alpha)\neq j(\beta)\wedge d(U_{j(\alpha)},U_{j(\beta)})=S_{0}.

(220)

For simplicity, we introduce
\simeq
as

\displaystyle a\simeq b\iff a-b=O(h^{\infty})e^{S_{0}/h}.

(221)

In order to calculate the integration, we need to introduce some geometry settings.
Let
E^{(a)}
be an elliptic region, namely,

E^{(a)}=\{x\in\mathbb{R}^{d},d(U_{j(\alpha)})+d(U_{j(\beta)})\leq S_{0}+a\}.

(222)

The integration (
207
) for
x\notin E^{(a)}
gives a contribution
\simeq 0
. So later we can only consider integration near
E^{(a)}
.
The parameter
a
is chosen to be sufficiently small so that

E^{(a)}\subset M_{j(\alpha)}\cup M_{j(\beta)}
and there is no other wells in
E^{(a)}
.
Then, there will always exist such an open set
\Omega
with smooth boundary satisfying

U_{j(\alpha)}\subset\Omega,\penalty\ U_{j(\beta)}\cap\overline{\Omega}=\varnothing,\penalty\ E^{(a)}\cap\overline{\Omega}\subset M_{j(\alpha)},\penalty\ E^{(a)}\cap\Omega^{\complement}\subset M_{j(\beta)}.

(223)

Figure 11:
Illustration of the geometric relations in Eq. (
223
).

Let
\Gamma=\partial\Omega\cap E^{(a)}
, which is compact in
M_{j(\alpha)}\cap M_{j(\beta)}
.
Then, we make
E^{(2a)}
satisfy the properties as well by choosing proper
a
.
To simplify the calculation, we introduce
\chi_{E^{(a)}}\in C^{\infty}_{0}(M)
which equal to
1
in
E^{(a)}
and has support in

E^{(2a)}
. The following relations hold

\displaystyle\mathrm{Supp}\chi_{E^{(a)}}\cap\overline{\Omega}\subset M_{j(\alpha)},\penalty\ \mathrm{Supp}\chi_{E^{(a)}}\cap\Omega^{\complement}\subset M_{j(\beta)}

(224)

Therefore, we know
\chi_{j(\alpha)}=1
on
\mathrm{Supp}\chi_{E^{(a)}}\cap\overline{\Omega}
and
\chi_{j(\beta)}=1
on
\mathrm{Supp}\chi_{E^{(a)}}\cap\Omega^{\complement}
, leading to
the second
\simeq
in the following equation:

\displaystyle W_{\alpha\beta}

\displaystyle\simeq h^{2}\int\chi_{E^{(a)}}\chi_{j(\alpha)}(\phi_{\beta}\nabla\phi_{\alpha}-\phi_{\alpha}\nabla\phi_{\beta})\cdot\nabla\chi_{j(\beta)}\mathrm{d}x

(225)

\displaystyle\simeq h^{2}\int_{\Omega}\chi_{E^{(a)}}(\phi_{\beta}\nabla\phi_{\alpha}-\phi_{\alpha}\nabla\phi_{\beta})\cdot\nabla\chi_{j(\beta)}\mathrm{d}x.

(226)

Use the Green formula, we have

W_{\alpha\beta}\simeq h^{2}\int_{\partial\Omega}\chi_{E^{(a)}}\chi_{j(\beta)}(\phi_{\beta}\nabla\phi_{\alpha}-\phi_{\alpha}\nabla\phi_{\beta})\cdot(-\boldsymbol{n})dS_{\Gamma}-h^{2}\int_{\Omega}\nabla\cdot(\chi_{E^{(a)}}(\phi_{\beta}\nabla\phi_{\alpha}-\phi_{\alpha}\nabla\phi_{\beta}))\chi_{j(\beta)}\mathrm{d}x,

(227)

where
\boldsymbol{n}
is the unit normal on
\partial\Omega
riented into
\Omega
and
dS_{\Gamma}
is the induced measure on
\partial\Omega
.
With
Assumption
A.5
, the second term in the right hand side of (
227
) is of
O(h^{\infty}e^{-S_{0}/h})
, which means

W_{\alpha\beta}\simeq h^{2}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\phi_{\alpha}\frac{\partial\phi_{\beta}}{\partial n}-\phi_{\beta}\frac{\partial\phi_{\alpha}}{\partial n}\right)dS_{\Gamma}.

(228)

And, we can have:

Proposition A.5

(Theorem 4.3.4 of
Helffer 1988
)
.

By adding
Assumption
A.5
,
Proposition
A.4
can be improved to the following:
the matrix
P_{|\mathcal{F}}
in the basis
\{e_{\alpha}\}
is given by

\mathrm{diag}(\mu_{\alpha})+\widetilde{W}_{\alpha\beta}+O(h^{\infty})e^{S_{0}/h},

(229)

with

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{\alpha\beta}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}h^{2}\int_{\Gamma}\left(\phi_{\alpha}\frac{\partial\phi_{\beta}}{\partial n}-\phi_{\beta}\frac{\partial\phi_{\alpha}}{\partial n}\right)dS_{\Gamma},\penalty\ \mathrm{if}\penalty\ d(U_{j(\alpha)},U_{j(\beta)})=S_{0},\\
0,\penalty\ \mathrm{otherwise}.\end{array}\right.

Now, we just need the knowledge of
\phi_{\alpha}
in a neighborhood of
\Gamma
to get the explicit form of
P_{|\mathcal{F}}
.
WKB method in
Appendix
A.2.1
can be used to estimate
\phi_{\alpha}
in the bottom (local ground states),
whose validity is secured by

Assumption A.6
.

0=\min f<\lim_{|x|\to\infty}f,

(232)

f^{-1}(0)=U_{1}\cup U_{2}\ldots\cup U_{N},

(233)

U_{j}={x_{j}}\penalty\ \mathrm{is\penalty\ a\penalty\ point,}\penalty\ \mathrm{and}\penalty\ \nabla^{2}V(x_{j})>0\penalty\ \mathrm{for}\penalty\ j=1,\ldots,N.

(234)

That is, local minima are global minima.
We choose
I(h)
to be small enough such that
there is a integer
N^{+}
and for
j=1,\ldots,N^{+}
,

P_{M_{j}}
has exactly one eigenvalue in
I(h)
but for

j=N^{+}+1,\ldots,N
,
P_{M_{j}}
has no eigenvalue near

I(h)
.
Resonance happens between wells
\{x_{j}\}
for
j=1,\ldots,N^{+}
.
We now just have to compute
\widetilde{W}_{jk}
for
j\neq k
,
d(x_{j},x_{k})=S_{0}
and
j,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,N^{+}\}
.

We know from
Lemma
A.1
that the local eigenstates have the form

\phi_{j}(x)=h^{-d/4}a^{(j)}(x)e^{-d(x,x_{j})/h},

(235)

and satisfy

(P-E^{(j)})\phi_{j}(x)=O(h^{\infty})e^{-d(x,x_{j})/h}.

(236)

In the semi-classical analysis, the function
a^{(j)}(x)
can be expanded as

a^{(j)}(x)\approx\sum_{k=0}^{\infty}a_{k}^{(j)}(x)h^{k}.

(237)

Insert this form to (
A.5
), we have

\widetilde{W}_{jk}=-h^{1-d/2}\int_{\Gamma_{jk}}\left[a^{(j)}a^{(k)}\Big(\frac{\partial d(x,x_{k})}{\partial n}-\frac{\partial d(x,x_{j})}{\partial n}\Big)+O(h)\right]e^{-\frac{d_{jk}(x)}{h}}dS_{\Gamma_{jk}},

(238)

where
d_{jk}(x)=d(x,x_{j})+d(x,x_{k})
.

Assumption A.7
.

There are a finite number of geodesics
\gamma_{jk}^{(l)}

of length
S_{0}
joining
x_{j}
and
x_{k}
, where
l\in\Lambda_{jk}
is the index denoting which geodesic it is.
Let
\Gamma_{jk}^{(l)}\subset\Omega_{j}^{\complement}\cap\Omega_{k}
be a smooth hypersurface intersecting

\gamma_{jk}^{(l)}
such that there would be a point
x_{jk}^{(l)}\in\gamma_{jk}^{(l)}\cap\Omega_{j}\cap\Omega_{k}
being the only point in
\overline{\Gamma}_{jk}^{(l)}\cap\gamma_{jk}^{(l)}
.

Proposition A.6

(Theorem 4.4.6 and 4.4.7 in
Helffer 1988
)
.

For
j,k\in\{1,2,\ldots,N^{+}\}
and
d(x_{j},x_{k})=S_{0}
,
there is a constant
C>0
such that

\frac{1}{C}h^{1/2}\leq-\widetilde{W}_{jk}e^{S_{0}/h}\leq Ch^{1-d/2}.

(239)

More specifically, we have

\widetilde{W}_{jk}=-h^{1/2}(\sum_{m}b^{(m)}_{jk}h^{m})e^{-S_{0}/h},

(240)

with

b^{(0)}_{jk}=2(2\pi)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\sum_{l\in\Lambda_{jk}}\sqrt{\frac{f(x_{jk}^{(l)})}{\mathrm{det}\Big(\nabla^{2}d_{jk}(x_{jk}^{(l)})\Big)}}a^{(j)}_{0}(x_{jk}^{(l)})a^{(k)}_{0}(x_{jk}^{(l)}).

(241)

Proof.

We would use the semi-classical hypothesis to
calculate the leading term of (
238
).
Note that only regions near
x^{(l)_{jk}},\penalty\ l\in\Lambda_{jk}
have contribution to the leading term:

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{jk}=-h^{1-d/2}e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}}\sum_{l\in\Lambda_{jk}}\int_{B_{l}(h)}\left[a_{0}^{(j)}(x_{jk}^{(l)})a^{(k)}_{0}(x_{jk}^{(l)})\Big(\frac{\partial d(x,x_{k})}{\partial n}-\frac{\partial d(x,x_{j})}{\partial n}\Big)+O(h)\right]e^{-\frac{d_{jk}(x)-S_{0}}{h}}dS_{\Gamma_{jk}},

(242)

where
B_{l}(h)\subset\Gamma_{jk}
is a neighborhood of
x^{(l)}_{jk}
. We can set
B_{l}(h)\to\{x^{(l)}_{jk}\},\penalty\ (h\to 0)
.
Recall that
x^{(l)}_{jk}
is a minimum of
d_{jk}(x)
in
\Gamma_{jk}
,
in the neighborhood of
x^{(l)}_{jk}
, we have

\displaystyle e^{-\frac{d_{jk}(x)-S_{0}}{h}}=e^{-\frac{X^{\top}\nabla^{2}d_{jk}(x_{jk}^{(l)})X}{2h}}+\mathrm{h.o.t},

(243)

where
X=\{x_{1},\ldots,x_{d-1}\}
is the local coordinate of
\Gamma_{jk}
centered in
x_{jk}^{(l)}
and h.o.t denotes higher order terms.
Examine the integral near
x_{jk}^{(l)}
:

\displaystyle\int_{B_{l}(h)}\Big(\frac{\partial d(x,x_{k})}{\partial n}-\frac{\partial d(x,x_{j})}{\partial n}\Big)e^{-\frac{d_{jk}(x)-S_{0}}{h}}dS_{\Gamma_{jk}}

\displaystyle=\int 2\sqrt{f(x_{jk}^{(l)})}e^{-\frac{X^{\top}\nabla^{2}d_{jk}(x_{jk}^{(l)})X}{2h}}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{d-1}+\mathrm{h.o.t}

\displaystyle=2\sqrt{f(x_{jk}^{(l)})}\sqrt{\frac{(2\pi h)^{d-1}}{\mathrm{det}\Big(\nabla^{2}d_{jk}(x_{jk}^{(l)})\Big)}}+\mathrm{h.o.t}.

(244)

So that we have

\displaystyle\widetilde{W}_{jk}=-h^{1/2}e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}}\sum_{l\in\Lambda_{jk}}2(2\pi)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}a_{0}^{(j)}(x_{jk}^{(l)})a^{(k)}_{0}(x_{jk}^{(l)})\sqrt{\frac{f(x_{jk}^{(l)})}{\mathrm{det}\Big(\nabla^{2}d_{jk}(x_{jk}^{(l)})\Big)}}+\mathrm{h.o.t}.

(245)

∎

Theorem A.1

(Energy gap)
.

Assume the assumptions for
Proposition
A.6
are satisfied and
N^{+}=N
.
The minimal energy gap
\Delta E
of
P_{|\mathcal{F}}
, i.e., the minimal absolute difference between unequal eigenvalues of
P_{|\mathcal{F}}
,
is given by
\Delta E=\sqrt{h}(b+O(h))e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}}

where
b>0
is a constant that depends only on the potential
f
.

Proof.

Because of
Proposition
A.5
, we have now

\displaystyle P_{|\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{diag}(\mu_{j})+(\widetilde{W}_{ij})+O(h^{\infty})e^{S_{0}/h}.

(246)

According to
Proposition
A.6
,
\widetilde{W}_{ij}
is of the form

-h^{1/2}(b_{ij}+O(h))e^{-S_{0}/h}
, where
b_{ij}
depends only on
i,j
, and the potential
f
.
By
Assumption
A.5
,
|\mu_{j}-\mu_{i}|=O(h^{\infty})e^{-S_{0}/h}
,
so we have

\displaystyle P_{|\mathcal{F}}=\mathrm{diag}(\mu)-h^{1/2}(b_{ij}+O(h))e^{-S_{0}/h}+O(h^{\infty})e^{S_{0}/h},

(247)

where
\mu
is chosen to be one
\mu_{j}
.
The eigenvalues of
P_{|\mathcal{F}}
should be of the form

\displaystyle E_{j}=\mu+h^{1/2}(b_{j}+O(h))e^{-S_{0}/h}+O(h^{\infty})e^{S_{0}/h},

(248)

where
b_{j}
are constants depending only on
f
and
j
.
Therefore, we have the energy gap
\Delta E=\min_{E_{j}\neq E_{i}}|E_{j}-E_{i}|
as

\displaystyle\Delta E=h^{1/2}(b+O(h))e^{-S_{0}/h}+O(h^{\infty})e^{S_{0}/h}=h^{1/2}(b+O(h))e^{-S_{0}/h},

(249)

where
b
is a constant only related to
f
.
∎

Appendix B
Auxiliary Algorithmic Techniques for Quantum Tunneling Walks

In this section, we introduce two quantum algorithmic techniques that are necessary for obtaining our results. One is quantum simulation of the Schrödinger equation (
Appendix
B.1
), and the other is initial state preparation (
Appendix
B.2
).

B.1
Quantum Simulation of the Schrödinger Equation

The Schrödinger equation we want to simulate is

\displaystyle i\frac{\partial}{\partial t}\Phi=(-h^{2}\Delta+f(x))\Phi.

(250)

We regard the simulation of (
250
) as solving this special partial differential equation (PDE) by quantum algorithms.
Following
Costa et al. 2019
;
Zhang et al. 2021a
, we work in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and use the finite difference
method which discretizes the space into
grids with side-length
a
.
In this way, any function
\phi
can be viewed as a vector
and the Hamiltonian
H=-h^{2}\Delta+f

becomes a matrix.
For instance, in the one-dimensional case,

\phi_{j}
is the function value of
\phi
on the
j
th grid which forms a
vector,
-h^{2}\Delta
is discretized to be
-\frac{h^{2}}{a^{2}}L
where

L
is the Laplacian matrix of the graph of the grids (whose off-diagonal entries are
-1
for connected grids and zero otherwise, and diagonal entries are the degree of the grids), giving

\displaystyle(\Delta\phi)_{j}\approx\frac{1}{a^{2}}(L\phi)_{j}=\frac{\phi_{j+1}-2\phi_{j}+\phi_{j-1}}{a^{2}},

(251)

and
f
in the Hamiltonian
H
is discretized to be a matrix whose
j
th diagonal entry is the value of
f
at the
j
th grid. Here the approximation
\approx
follows the definition in Eq. (
13
) and the LHS and RHS of (
251
) is equal under the limit
a\to 0
.
The step of discretization would bring errors which can be
controlled by making
a
smaller. For convenience, we define
A:=-\frac{h^{2}}{a^{2}}L_{k}
,
where
L_{k}
is the Laplacian matrix associated to the
k
th order finite difference method
Childs et al. 2021
, and define
B
the diagonal matrix discretizing
f
.
We use the matrix
\hat{H}:=A+B
to model the Hamiltonian of the Schrödinger equation under discretization.
To simulate the time evolution governed by
\hat{H}
, we use the following result:

Lemma B.1

(Lemma 6 in
Low and Wiebe 2019
)
.

Let
A
,
B\in\mathbb{C}^{n\times n}
be two time-independent Hermitian matrices such that
\|A\|\leq\alpha_{A}
and
\|B\|\leq\alpha_{B}
. Then, the time evolution operator
e^{-i(A+B)t}
can be approximated up to error
\epsilon
with

\displaystyle O\left(\alpha_{B}t\frac{\log(\alpha_{B}t/\epsilon)}{\log\log(\alpha_{B}t/\epsilon)}\right)

(252)

quires to the unitary oracle
O_{B}
.

Remark B.1
.

The
\epsilon
in
Lemma
B.1
originally denotes the error bound quantified by
the spectral norm. Namely, let
\mathcal{T}_{t}
be the real operator obtained,
the error
\|\mathcal{T}_{t}-e^{-i(A+B)t}\|\leq\epsilon
.
For any initial state
\left|\Phi(0)\right\rangle
,
\left|\Phi(t)\right\rangle=e^{-i(A+B)t}\left|\Phi(0)\right\rangle

is the state we want to prepare and
|\tilde{\Phi}(t)\rangle=\mathcal{T}_{t}\left|\Phi(0)\right\rangle
the state we can prepare.
Nevertheless, It is straightforward to have
\|\tilde{\Phi}(t)-\Phi(t)\|\leq\epsilon
, which implies
\epsilon
that can be understood as the error under
\ell^{2}
norm.

Remark B.2
.

Note the Lemma 6 in
Low and Wiebe 2019
gives the number of queries to the oracle HAM-T.
Because the construction of HAM-T only needs one query to
O_{B}
,
Lemma
B.1
follows.
Moreover, if we specify
B
as the diagonal matrix discretizing the objective function
f
, one quantum query to
O_{B}
can be implemented by one query to the quantum evaluation oracle
U_{f}
given by (
11
).

Classical and quantum methods approximating (
250
) need to discretize the time.
The time step
\Delta t
for classical methods to maintain convergence
is of the order
1/\|\hat{H}\|
.
Therefore, steps or queries needed for evolving a time
t
is about
t/\Delta t\sim t\|\hat{H}\|
. The term
\|\hat{H}\|
can be understood as the maximum energy, indicating that simulating higher energy states accurately costs more.
This intuition is partially true for quantum simulation.
By simulating the Hamiltonian in the interaction picture, the query complexity of

Lemma
B.1
depends on the maximum potential energy instead of the maximum energy because
\|B\|\leq\|f\|_{\infty}
.
With
Lemma
B.1
on simulating
\hat{H}
, we now determine the number of queries needed to simulate
H
.

Lemma B.2

(Slightly modified version from Lemma 2 in
Zhang et al. 2021a
)
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
be bounded in a domain
\Omega\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
, consider the Schrödinger equation (
250
)
restricted in the domain
\Omega
with periodic boundary condition
or Dirichlet boundary condition (value of the wave function vanishes at the boundary).
14
14

14

The set
\Omega
should be appropriate such that the Schrödinger equation is solvable. For the purpose of optimization,
\Omega
should be sufficiently large to contain all minima of interest, and the wave function can hardly hit the boundary
\partial\Omega
. For consistency in mathematics, the initial state should be in
H^{1}_{0}(\Omega)\cap H^{2}(\Omega)
(see
Definition
A.6
) if we use the Dirichlet boundary condition.

Having the quantum evaluation oracle
U_{f}
in (
11
)
and an initial state adapting to the boundary condition at
t=0
, the evolution
of (
250
) for time
t
can be
simulated up to
L^{2}
norm error
\epsilon
using

\displaystyle O\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t\frac{\log(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}{\log\log(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}\right)

(253)

queries to
U_{f}
.

Proof of
Lemma
B.2
.

There are two sources that producing error: the first comes from discretizing the spatial domain (approximating the Hamiltonian
H
by the matrix
\hat{H}
) and the second comes from simulating the Hamiltonian matrix

\hat{H}
with the help of
Lemma
B.1
.

Let the initial wave function be
\Phi(0,x)
and the accurate wave function after time
t
be
\Phi(t,x)=e^{-iHt}\Phi(0,x)
.
By discretization, let
\{x_{j}\}_{j\in I}
be the grid points where
I
is the set of index,
we then obtain a vector
\hat{\Phi}(0)
whose
j
th entry is the value
\Phi(0,x_{j})
.
Denote the vector
\hat{\Phi}(t)=e^{-i\hat{H}t}\hat{\Phi}(0)
,
15
15

15

The boundary condition can be satisfied by
setting some entries at the edge of the matrix
A
in
\hat{H}
as 0 or 1/
\Delta x^{2}
,
which is a standard process in solving differential equations by finite difference methods and can be found in extensive classical literature.
we can rebuild a function

\hat{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,x)
by setting the function value on the
j
th grid (a hypercube with side-length
a
) to be the
j
th entry of
\hat{\Phi}(t)
.
By setting
1/a=\mathrm{poly}(d)\mathrm{poly}(\log(2/\epsilon))
, it can be made that
\|\hat{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,\cdot)-\Phi(t,x)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\epsilon/2

Childs et al. 2021
.
Since
\|B\|\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
,
Lemma
B.1
permits an operator
\mathcal{T}_{t}
simulating
e^{-i\hat{H}t}
with

\displaystyle Q(t,\epsilon)=O\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t\frac{\log(2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}{\log\log(2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}\right)

(254)

queries to
O_{B}
, such that
\|\tilde{\Phi}(t)-\hat{\Phi}(t)\|\leq\epsilon/2
where
\tilde{\Phi}(t):=\mathcal{T}_{t}\hat{\Phi}(0)
(see
Remark
B.1
).
Similar to rebuilding
\hat{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,x)
from the vector

\hat{\Phi}(t)
, we can rebuild a function
\tilde{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,x)
from
\tilde{\Phi}(t)
and

\|\tilde{\Phi}(t)-\hat{\Phi}(t)\|\leq\epsilon/2
is equivalent to
\|\tilde{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,\cdot)-\hat{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\epsilon/2
.
Finally, by the triangle inequality,

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,\cdot)-\Phi(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\|\tilde{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,\cdot)-\hat{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,\cdot)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}+\|\hat{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,\cdot)-\Phi(t,x)\|_{L^{2}(\Omega)}\leq\epsilon.

(255)

In the present case, a query to
O_{B}
can be implemneted by
one query to the quantum evaluation oracle
U_{f}
(
Remark
B.2
). Note that

\displaystyle\frac{\log(2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}{\log\log(2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}\leq\frac{1+\log(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}{\log\log(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}\leq\frac{2\log(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}{\log\log(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)},

(256)

we can also write

\displaystyle Q(t,\epsilon)=O\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t\frac{\log(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}{\log\log(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}t/\epsilon)}\right).

(257)

In summary, using
Q(t,\epsilon)
queries to
U_{f}
, we are able to
obtain a wave function
\tilde{\Phi}^{\vee}(t,x)

close to the actual solution
\Phi(t,x)
up to error
\epsilon
,
which completes the proof.
∎

Remark B.3
.

The discretization method introduced indicates that every grid is a hypercube with side-length
a
.
If the boundary
\Omega
is curved, grids fitting the boundary and the boundary condition can be irregular. In this case, discretization of the
Laplacian, or equivalently the form of the matrix
A
, will be different. We clarify that
Lemma
B.2
is still true for irregular grids since
grids do not affect the fact that
\|B\|\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(\Omega)}
.

Up to now, we introduce quantum simulation in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
or more precisely, a subset
\Omega
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
We further claim that for smooth (compact) manifolds that can be embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, the query complexity presented in
Lemma
B.2
should still be valid.
On such manifolds, say
M
, we can use discretization methods in
\mathbb{R}^{d}

to approximate the discretization in
M
.
Note that the Laplacian should be replaced by the Laplace–Beltrami operator, adding difficulties for construct the matrix
A
.
However, since
A
does not affect query complexity and
B
will always be a diagonal matrix and
\|B\|\leq\|f\|_{L^{\infty}(M)}
,
the form of (
253
) should be the same.
In general, quantum simulation on manifolds are interesting topics, deserving further studies.

Note that
Lemma
B.1
we use bound the error of states by bounding the error of operators.
This may not provide the possible minimal queries needed if the initial state is in a low energy subspace which is exactly the case for QTW. Quantum simulation in a low energy subspace intuitively lowers the query complexity bound
Şahinoğlu and Somma 2021
,
indicating larger speedups of QTW.

B.2
Initial State Preparation

QTW is a quantum simulation algorithm making use of quantum tunneling. To fulfill the tunneling phenomenon, not only should the potential function
f(x)
satisfy
some assumptions (as is presented in
Section
2.3
), but also the
initial state should be in a low-energy subspace. For completeness, we introduce in this appendix possible ways for preparing initial states under different scenarios.

For a landscape
f(x)
satisfying assumptions in
Section
2.3
,

Proposition
A.3
in
Appendix
A.2.3

guarantees that for sufficiently small
h
, local eigenstates with cut-offs (see
Definition
A.8
)
can span a space
\mathcal{E}
that is close to
\mathcal{F}
, a low-energy subspace of the Hamiltonian
H=-h^{2}\Delta+f
.
Moreover, orthonormalized local eigenstates (see
Definition
A.10
) can span the space
\mathcal{F}
.
Note that we only consider tunneling between local ground states,
hence the above claim implies that orthonormalized local ground states can span the space
\mathcal{F}
.

Assumption
2.4
supposes that there are
N
global minima (wells) of interest.
Therefore, we can label the minima by
j\in\{1,\ldots,N\}
and denote
the orthonormalized local ground state corresponding to the
j
th minima by
\left|j\right\rangle
.
Let
\left|E_{j}\right\rangle
(
j=1,2,\ldots
) be eigenstates of
H
with eigenvalues
E_{j}
, respectively.
Mathematically, we can write

\displaystyle\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{span}\{\left|j\right\rangle\}_{j=1}^{N}=\mathrm{span}\{\left|E_{j}\right\rangle\}_{j=1}^{N}.

(258)

The goal is to prepare a state (nearly) in the subspace
\mathcal{F}
such that QTW can be initiated.

First, we explore a simple scenario where we have previous knowledge of one minimum.

Local ground state preparation.

With the knowledge of one minimum, say the
j
th one, an efficient way of state preparation is to
prepare or approximate the local ground state corresponding to the
j
th minimum.
In practice,
\left|j\right\rangle
has no explicit form and we need to
prepare the original local ground state
\left|\phi_{j}\right\rangle
(see
Definition
A.7
).
Restating
Definition
A.7
in the present case,

M_{j}
(rigorously given by (
187
))
is a bounded region that contains only the
j
th minimum
and
\left|\phi_{j}\right\rangle
is the ground state of
H_{M_{j}}
which
is the Dirichlet realization of
H
in
M_{j}
(see
Definition
A.6
).
Regardless of the rigorous mathematical definition,
the function
\phi_{j}=\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{\phi_{j}}\right\rangle\colon M_{j}\to\mathbb{C}

could be understood as the function
\phi
satisfying
(-h^{2}\Delta+f)\phi=E\phi
and
\phi_{|\partial M_{j}}=0
whose
E
is the smallest.

One way to prepare (approximate)
\left|\phi_{j}\right\rangle
is through adiabatic quantum computing restricted in

M_{j}
(some set
\Omega_{j}\subset M_{j}
).
Adiabatic quantum computing presents such a picture, for a time-dependent Hamiltonian
H(t)
,
if the initial state
\psi(0)
is the ground state of
H(0)
and
H(t)
changes sufficiently slow, the system will approximately stay at the ground state of
H(t)
at
t
.
For the purpose of preparing ground state of
H_{M_{j}}
(
H_{\Omega_{j}}
),
we can let
H(0)
be some known Hamiltonian and let
H(T)=H_{M_{j}}
(
H_{\Omega_{j}}
).
Sufficiently slowly changing
H(t)
is equivalent to a sufficiently large
T
.
Let
G(t)
be the fundamental gap of
H(t)
, i.e., the difference between the second smallest and smallest eigenvalues of
H(t)
,
time for state preparation can be bounded as follows.

Lemma B.3

(Informal quantum adiabatic theorem
Duan 2020
)
.

Let
\phi(t)
be be the unit eigenstate of
H(t)
and
\left\langle{\partial_{t}\phi(t)}\right|\left.\!{\phi(t)}\right\rangle=0
. Simulating the time dependent Hamiltonian
H(t)
when
\psi(0)=\phi(0)
,
we can obtain
\psi(T)
such that
\|\psi(T)-\phi(t)\|\leq\epsilon
if

\displaystyle T\geq\frac{1}{\epsilon}\left(\frac{4\|\partial_{t}H\|+2\|\partial_{tt}H\|}{G_{m}^{2}}+\frac{20\|\partial_{t}H\|^{2}}{G_{m}^{3}}\right),

(259)

where
G_{m}=\inf_{0\leq t\leq T}G(t)
.

Intuitively, the time cost is bounded by the inverse of the minimal fundamental gap.
For simplicity, we study
H_{\Omega_{j}}
for small convex
\Omega_{j}

where
f_{|\Omega_{j}}
is also weakly convex.
The ground state of
H_{\Omega_{j}}
can be close to that of
H_{M_{j}}
if
h
is small.
By
Andrews and Clutterbuck 2011
, fundamental gaps of Hamiltonians restricted in
\Omega_{j}

is
\Omega(h^{2}/D^{2})
where
D
is the diameter of
\Omega_{j}
.
As is shown by
Theorem
1.1
, time cost of
QTW is dominated by
1/\Delta E
where
\Delta E=O(e^{-S_{0}/h})=O(h^{\infty})
is also an energy gap.
Therefore, for small
h
that ensuring tunneling and high accuracy,

1/\Delta E
is much larger than the inverse of any fundamental gaps of Hamiltonians restricted in
\Omega_{j}
, giving that local ground state preparation is much easier than QTW.

Classical methods could also help to prepare local ground states.
Still given the
j
th minimum
x_{j}
, we can use classical sampling to
learn the Hessian matrix at
x_{j}
,
\nabla^{2}f(x_{j})
.
Then, we can directly put the following wave function centered at
x_{j}
.

\displaystyle\phi(x)=\frac{\big(\det\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(x_{j})}\big)^{1/4}}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/4}}e^{-\frac{(x-x_{j})^{\top}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(x_{j})}(x-x_{j})}{2\sqrt{2}h}}.

(260)

Note that if
f
is quadratic, the
\phi(x)
in (
260
) will be the ground state precisely.
Using the ground state under quadratic landscape to approximate the actual ground state is actually the idea of WKB approximation in
Appendix
A.2.1
.
The smaller
h
is, the more accurate the approximation of (
260
) can be.
In addition, Gaussian wave packets can be efficiently sampled on quantum computers
Kitaev and Webb 2008
;
Rattew et al. 2021
.
It is still true that QTW takes more time than initial state preparation.

Local ground state preparation is convenient because it suffices to focus on a neighborhood of one minimum. However, it also has drawback as we cannot ensure that the state we obtain is in
\mathcal{F}
. Detailed error estimation is needed in future work.
16
16

16

To be specific,
\left|j\right\rangle
is in
\mathcal{F}
but we cannot prepare; what we can approximately prepare is
\left|\phi_{j}\right\rangle
.
Definition
A.7
,
Definition
A.8
, and
Definition
A.10
together give an estimation that
\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{j}\right\rangle=\phi_{j}+O(e^{-S_{0}/h})
.

General initial state preparation.

Next, we consider the general scenario where we do not have any priori knowledge on any minimum.

A straightforward way is to use classical algorithms (e.g., SGD) or quantum algorithms to find one minimum first and then apply above procedure of preparing local ground states.
Besides, we present a new idea that can directly yield a
state near the subspace
\mathcal{F}
.
The method is based on a generalized version of the quantum adiabatic theorem. Define
G_{\mathcal{F}}:=E_{N+1}-E_{N}
as the fundamental gap of space
\mathcal{F}
.
The proof of
Lemma
B.3
suggests that
as long as the total time
T

is long enough respective to the inverse of the fundamental gap of space
\mathcal{F}
, the system can always be near the space
\mathcal{F}
. See also
Boixo and Somma 2010
.
The state
\left|E_{N+1}\right\rangle
is orthogonal to
\mathcal{F}
as
\mathcal{F}=\mathrm{span}\{E_{j}\}_{j=1}^{N}
.
Hence,
\left|E_{N+1}\right\rangle
is at least a superposition of local first excited states
such that
G_{\mathcal{F}}
has a similar value to the gap between local ground energy and local first excited energy.
Then, for any landscape
f
satisfying assumptions in
Section
2.3
,
we can prepare states in the low-energy subspace
\mathcal{F}
with time
\sim\mathrm{poly}(1/G_{\mathcal{F}})
.

In summary, we provide several ways of preparing the desired initial states under different conditions. In principle, discussions on initial state preparation also suggest potential hybrid quantum-classical algorithms and potential quantum simulation algorithms containing both adiabatic quantum computing and QTW, which are of independent interest.

Appendix C
Technical Details for Quantum Tunneling Walks

C.1
Details of quantum mixing time

C.1.1
Proof of
Lemma
3.1

Lemma
3.1
aims to bound the minimum time
\tau
enabling
\|\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\|_{1}\leq\epsilon
(i.e., the mixing time).

Proof.

Straightforward calculations yield

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\|_{1}

\displaystyle=\int\left|\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{1-e^{-i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}}{i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}\langle x|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|x\rangle\right|\mathrm{d}x

(261)

\displaystyle\leq\int\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}\left|\langle x|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|x\rangle\right|\mathrm{d}x

(262)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j^{\prime}\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{j}\right\rangle\left\langle{j^{\prime}}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}x.

(263)

Because of
Assumption
2.4
in
Section
2
which can lead to
Assumption
A.5
, for
j\neq j^{\prime}
, we have

\langle x|j\rangle\langle j^{\prime}|x\rangle=O(h^{-N^{\prime}}e^{-\frac{d(x_{j},x)+d(x_{j^{\prime}},x)}{h}})\leq O(h^{-N^{\prime}}e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}})=O(h^{\infty}),

(264)

where
N^{\prime}>0
is some constant.
Therefore,

\int|\langle x|j\rangle\langle j^{\prime}|x\rangle|\mathrm{d}x=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}1,\quad j=j^{\prime},\\
O(h^{\infty}),\quad j\neq j^{\prime}.\end{array}\right.

(265)

By the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality,

\displaystyle|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j^{\prime}\rangle|\leq\frac{1}{2}(|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j^{\prime}\rangle|^{2}),

(266)

such that,

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j^{\prime}\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{j}\right\rangle\left\langle{j^{\prime}}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}x

(267)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|\frac{1}{2}(|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j^{\prime}\rangle|^{2})

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{j}\right\rangle\left\langle{j^{\prime}}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}x.

(268)

Splitting the sum on
j
and
j^{\prime}
, we have

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}\sum_{j=1}^{n}|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|\frac{1}{2}(|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|^{2})

\displaystyle+\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}}|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|\frac{1}{2}(|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j^{\prime}\rangle|^{2})|O(h^{\infty})|

(269)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|

\displaystyle+\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|(N-1)|O(h^{\infty})|

(270)

\displaystyle=\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|[1+(N-1)|O(h^{\infty})|].

(271)

One of the sufficient conditions to obtain (
66
) is that

\displaystyle\tau\geq\frac{2}{\epsilon}\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|}[1+(N-1)|O(h^{\infty})|].

(272)

Since the mixing time is the minimum
\tau
for (
66
) to be valid, an upper bound of the mixing time is given by the above equation. In other words,

\displaystyle T_{\rm mix}=O\bigg(\frac{1}{\epsilon}\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|}[1+(N-1)|O(h^{\infty})|]\bigg)

(273)

Note the definition of
\Delta E
and use the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Delta E}\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Delta E}\sum_{k,k^{\prime}}\frac{1}{2}(|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle|^{2}+|\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|^{2})=\frac{N}{\Delta E},

(274)

which complete the proof of
Lemma
3.1
.
∎

C.1.2
Proof of
Lemma
3.2

Lemma
3.2
reveals the relation between probabilities
p(\infty,j)
for
j=1,\ldots,N
and the limit distribution
\mu_{\rm QTW}
.

Proof.

We further estimate
\mu_{\rm QTW}
as follows

\displaystyle\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)

\displaystyle=\sum_{j,j^{\prime}}\sum_{E_{k}=E_{k^{\prime}}}\langle x|j\rangle\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j^{\prime}\rangle\langle j^{\prime}|x\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}\sum_{E_{k}=E_{k^{\prime}}}|\langle x|j\rangle|^{2}\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle

\displaystyle\penalty\ \penalty\ \penalty\ +\sum_{j\neq j^{\prime}}\sum_{E_{k}=E_{k^{\prime}}}\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j^{\prime}\rangle\langle j^{\prime}|x\rangle\langle x|j\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{j}p(\infty,j)|\langle x|j\rangle|^{2}+O(h^{\infty}),

(275)

where the last equality uses (
264
) and

\displaystyle p(\infty,j)=\sum_{E_{k}=E_{k^{\prime}}}\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle.

(276)

Because
|\langle x|j\rangle|^{2}
is the position distribution of the state
|j\rangle
,
\mu_{\rm QTW}
can be roughly seen as a weighted sum of the distributions of local states when
h
is small enough, which is intuitive.
∎

C.1.3
Proof of
Lemma
3.3

Lemma
3.3
bounds the mixing time of quantum walks whose evolution is governed by the interaction matrix of QTW.

Proof.

By the definition of
p(\tau,j)
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}|p(\tau,j)-p(\infty,j)|

\displaystyle=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\left|\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{1-e^{-i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}}{i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j=1}^{N}\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|.

(277)

The Cauchy-Schwarz inequality gives

\displaystyle|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|\leq\frac{1}{2}(|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|^{2}),

(278)

and then,

\displaystyle\sum_{j=1}^{N}|p(\tau,j)-p(\infty,j)|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|\frac{1}{2}(|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|^{2})

\displaystyle=\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|.

(279)

The condition (
72
) is satisfied if

\displaystyle\tau\geq\frac{2}{\epsilon}\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|},

(280)

Therefore, an upper bound of the quantum walk mixing time
t_{\rm mix}
is

\displaystyle\frac{2}{\epsilon}\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{|\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle|}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|}\leq\frac{2N}{\epsilon\Delta E}.

(281)

The last inequality uses (
274
), which finishes the proof.
∎

C.2
Details of quantum hitting time

C.2.1
Proof of
Lemma
3.4

We introduce by
Lemma
3.4
the typical upper bound of hitting time for quantum walks
Childs et al. 2003
.

Proof.

The definition of
p(\tau,j)
gives

\displaystyle|p(\tau,j)-p(\infty,j)|

\displaystyle=\left|\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{1-e^{-i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}}{i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle\right|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|,

(282)

which naturally leads to

\displaystyle p(\tau,j)\geq p(\infty,j)-\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|.

(283)

The second term of the right hand side of the above equation can be bounded as

\displaystyle\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}

\displaystyle\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\Delta E\tau}\sum_{E_{k},E_{k^{\prime}}}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|

(284)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\Delta E\tau}\sum_{E_{k},E_{k^{\prime}}}(|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|^{2}+|\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle|^{2})=\frac{2}{\Delta E\tau}.

(285)

Here, the second inequality uses the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality.
Therefore, we have
p(\tau,j)\geq p(\infty,j)-2/\Delta E\tau
.
Because
t_{\rm hit}(j)
is defined as the lower bound of
\tau/p(\tau,j)
for all
\tau>0
. Define
\tau_{\epsilon}=2/\Delta E\epsilon
for
\epsilon<p(\infty,j)
, we must have

\displaystyle t_{\rm hit}(j)\leq\frac{\tau_{\epsilon}}{p(\tau_{\epsilon},j)}\leq\frac{2/\Delta E\epsilon}{p(\infty,j)-\epsilon}.

(286)

∎

C.2.2
Proof of
Lemma
3.5

For QTW, the hitting time is estimated by
Lemma
3.5
similar to
Lemma
3.4
.

Proof.

We first estimate the quantity
\int_{\Omega_{j}}\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)\mathrm{d}x
:

\displaystyle\big|\int_{\Omega_{j}}(\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(x))\mathrm{d}x\big|

\displaystyle\leq\int_{\Omega_{j}}\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}\left|\langle x|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|x\rangle\right|\mathrm{d}x

(287)

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{2}{|E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}}|\tau}\sum_{l,j^{\prime}}|\langle l|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j^{\prime}\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int_{\Omega_{j}}|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{l}\right\rangle\left\langle{j^{\prime}}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}x.

(288)

Note that
Assumption
A.5
is satisfied as assumed, for
l\neq j^{\prime}
, we have

\langle x|l\rangle\langle j^{\prime}|x\rangle=O(h^{-N^{\prime}}e^{-\frac{d(x_{j},x)+d(x_{j^{\prime}},x)}{h}})\leq O(h^{-N^{\prime}}e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}})=O(h^{\infty}),

(289)

where
N^{\prime}>0
is some constant.
Then, it is readily to have

\displaystyle\big|\int_{\Omega_{j}}(\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(x))\mathrm{d}x\big|

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2}{\Delta E\tau}\big[\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\sum_{l}|\langle l|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|l\rangle|

\displaystyle\qquad\times\int_{\Omega_{j}}|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{l}\right\rangle\left\langle{l}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}x+|O(h^{\infty})|\big].

(290)

According to
Corollary
A.1
, the integrals
\int_{\Omega_{j}}|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{j}\right\rangle\left\langle{j}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}x=1+O(h^{\infty})
and
\forall l\neq j,\penalty\ \int_{\Omega_{l}}|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{l}\right\rangle\left\langle{l}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}x=O(h^{\infty})
, which can yield

\displaystyle\big|\int_{\Omega_{j}}(\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(x))\mathrm{d}x\big|\leq\frac{2}{\Delta E\tau}\big[\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|+|O(h^{\infty})|\big].

(291)

Apply the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{E_{k},E_{k^{\prime}}}|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|

(292)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{2}\sum_{E_{k},E_{k^{\prime}}}(|\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle|^{2}+|\langle\Phi(0)|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\Phi(0)\rangle|^{2})=1,

(293)

and then

\displaystyle\big|\int_{\Omega_{j}}(\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(x))\mathrm{d}x\big|\leq\frac{2}{\Delta E\tau}(1+|O(h^{\infty})|).

(294)

So,
\int_{\Omega_{j}}\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)\mathrm{d}x
can be bounded as

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{j}}\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)\mathrm{d}x\geq\int_{\Omega_{j}}\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\mathrm{d}x-\frac{2}{\Delta E\tau}(1+|O(h^{\infty})|).

(295)

Use
Corollary
A.1
again, we get
\int_{\Omega_{j}}\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\mathrm{d}x=p(\infty,j)+O(\infty)
which completes the proof of (
80
).

Because
T_{\rm hit}(\Omega_{j})
is the lower bound of
\tau/\int_{\Omega_{j}}\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)\mathrm{d}x
for all
\tau>0
. Given that
\tau_{\epsilon}=2(1+|O(h^{\infty})|)/\Delta E\epsilon
(the term
O(h^{\infty})
should be the same as that in (
294
)) for
\epsilon<\int_{\Omega_{j}}\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\mathrm{d}x
, we must have

\displaystyle T_{\rm hit}(\Omega_{j})\leq\frac{\tau_{\epsilon}}{\int_{\Omega_{j}}\rho_{\rm QTW}(\tau,x)\mathrm{d}x}\leq\frac{2}{\Delta E\epsilon}\frac{1+|O(h^{\infty})|}{\int_{\Omega_{j}}\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\mathrm{d}x-\epsilon}.

(296)

∎

C.3
Details about tensor decomposition

C.3.1
Proof of
Lemma
3.6

Lemma
3.6
explores the eigenstates and eigenvalues of
H_{|\mathcal{F}}
given by (
84
).To prove
Lemma
3.6
, we first study a simpler matrix.

Lemma C.1
.

Consider matrices of the following form:

A=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mu&w&\cdots&w\\
w&\mu&w&\vdots\\
\vdots&w&\ddots&w\\
w&\cdots&w&\mu\\
\end{array}\right).

(297)

The eigenvalues are

E_{1}=\cdots=E_{n-1}=\mu-w,\penalty\ E_{n}=\mu+(n-1)w,

(298)

And the corresponding eigenstates can be given by

|E_{k}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{n}}\sum_{j}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{n}kj}|j\rangle,

(299)

Lemma
C.1
can be easily verified. To prove it, we use the standard way: eigenvalues are solved from
\mathrm{det}(E_{i}I-A)=0
, and then a orthonormal set of eigenstates can be solve from
(E_{i}I-A)\left|E_{i}\right\rangle=0
.
Now, we turn to the proof of
Lemma
3.6
:

Proof of
Lemma
3.6
.

By symmetry, we first introduce the new basis,

\displaystyle|j,S\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|j,+\rangle+|j,-\rangle),

\displaystyle|j,A\rangle

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(|j,+\rangle-|j,-\rangle).

(300)

Here,
S
and
A
refer to symmetric and anti-symmetric, respectively. In the basis

\{|1,S\rangle,\ldots,|d,S\rangle,

|1,A\rangle,\ldots,|d,A\rangle\}
,

H_{|\mathcal{F}}
should be changed to

\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc|cccc}\mu&2w&\cdots&2w&&&&\\
2w&\mu&\ddots&\vdots&&&&\\
\vdots&\ddots&\ddots&2w&&&&\\
2w&\cdots&2w&\mu&&&&\\
\hline\cr&&&&\mu&&&\\
&&&&&\mu&&\\
&&&&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&&&&\mu\\
\end{array}\right),

(301)

where the upper left block is
d\times d
and is of the case described by
Lemma
C.1
. So, we know that
d
eigenstates of
H_{|\mathcal{F}}
is given by

|E_{k}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\sum_{j}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{d}kj}|j,S\rangle,\penalty\ k=1,\ldots,d

(302)

And the corresponding eigenvalues are

E_{1}=\cdots=E_{d-1}=\mu-2w,\penalty\ E_{d}=\mu+2w(n-1).

(303)

Clearly,
|j,A\rangle,\penalty\ (j=1,\ldots,d)
form the left
d
eigenstates with the eigenvalue
\mu
.
∎

C.3.2
Proof of
Lemma
3.7

Lemma
3.7
estimates the mixing time of QTW on the landscape (
84
).

Proof.

First recall that

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm QTW}-\mu_{\rm QTW}

\displaystyle=\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{1-e^{-i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}}{i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau/}\langle x|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|x\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}\sum_{E_{k}\neq E_{k^{\prime}}}\frac{1-e^{-i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}}{i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}\langle\beta|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|\beta\rangle|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}+\frac{1}{w\tau}O(h^{\infty})e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{\beta}\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}}\frac{1-e^{-i(2dw)\tau}}{i2dw\tau}\langle\beta|E_{d}\rangle\langle E_{d}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|\beta\rangle|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}+\mathrm{c.c.}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\beta}\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{d+1,\ldots,2d\}\\
k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}\end{subarray}}\frac{1-e^{-i(2w)\tau}}{i2w\tau}\langle\beta|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|\beta\rangle|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}+\mathrm{c.c.}

\displaystyle\quad+\sum_{\beta}\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\{d+1,\ldots,2d\}}\frac{1-e^{-i(2(d-1)w)\tau}}{i2(d-1)w\tau}\langle\beta|E_{d}\rangle\langle E_{d}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|\beta\rangle|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}+\mathrm{c.c.}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{w\tau}O(h^{\infty})e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}}.

(304)

Here, the second equality is true because

\langle x|\mu\rangle\langle\nu|z\rangle\propto e^{-\frac{d_{x,\pi_{\mu}a_{j(\mu)}}+d_{x,\pi_{\nu}a_{j(\nu)}}}{h}}\leq e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}},

(305)

and the third equality uses the fact

f(E_{k},E_{k^{\prime}},\alpha,\beta)=f(E_{k^{\prime}},E_{k},\alpha,\beta)^{*},

(306)

where

f(E_{k},E_{k^{\prime}},\alpha,\beta)\equiv\frac{1-e^{-i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}}{i(E_{k}-E_{k^{\prime}})\tau}\langle\beta|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|\beta\rangle|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}.

(307)

and c.c. denote “complex conjugate." Now, we calculate
different terms separately using
Lemma
3.6
:

\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}}f(E_{d},E_{k}^{\prime},\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}}\frac{1-e^{-i(2dw)\tau}}{i2dw\tau}\frac{1}{(2d)^{2}}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{d}k^{\prime}(j(\alpha)-j(\beta))}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1-e^{-i2dw\tau}}{i2dw\tau}\frac{d-1}{(2d)^{2}}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2},\penalty\ j(\alpha)=j(\beta),\\
\frac{e^{-i2dw\tau}-1}{i2dw\tau}\frac{1}{(2d)^{2}}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2},\penalty\ j(\alpha)\neq j(\beta),\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{d+1,\ldots,2d\}\\
k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}\end{subarray}}f(E_{k},E_{k}^{\prime},\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{\begin{subarray}{c}k\in\{d+1,\ldots,2d\}\\
k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}\end{subarray}}\frac{1-e^{-i2w\tau}}{i2w\tau}\frac{\pi_{\alpha}\pi_{\beta}\delta_{j(\alpha),j(\beta)}\delta_{j(\alpha),k-d}}{4d}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{d}k^{\prime}(j(\alpha)-j(\beta))}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=\delta_{j(\alpha),j(\beta)}\frac{1-e^{-i2w\tau}}{i2w\tau}\pi_{\alpha}\pi_{\beta}\frac{d-1}{4d}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2},

(310)

\displaystyle\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\{d+1,\ldots,2d\}}f(E_{d},E_{k}^{\prime},\alpha,\beta)

\displaystyle=\sum_{k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}}\frac{1-e^{-i2(d-1)w\tau}}{i2(d-1)w\tau}\frac{1}{4d}\delta_{j(\alpha),j(\beta)}\delta_{j(\alpha),k^{\prime}-d}\pi_{\alpha}\pi_{\beta}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle=\frac{1-e^{-i2(d-1)w\tau}}{i2(d-1)w\tau}\frac{1}{4d}\delta_{j(\alpha),j(\beta)}\pi_{\alpha}\pi_{\beta}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}.

(311)

Substituting the above results in (
304
) gives

\displaystyle\rho_{\rm QTW}-\mu_{\rm QTW}

\displaystyle=\sum_{j(\beta)\neq j(\alpha)}-\frac{\sin(2dw\tau)}{dw\tau}\frac{1}{(2d)^{2}}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}+\sum_{j(\beta)=j(\alpha)}\frac{\sin(2dw\tau)}{dw\tau}\frac{d-1}{(2d)^{2}}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sin(2w\tau)}{w\tau}\frac{d-1}{4d}(|\langle x|j(\alpha),\pi_{\alpha}\rangle|^{2}-|\langle x|j(\alpha),-\pi_{\alpha}\rangle|^{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{\sin(2(d-1)w\tau)}{(d-1)w\tau}\frac{1}{4d}(|\langle x|j(\alpha),\pi_{\alpha}\rangle|^{2}-|\langle x|j(\alpha),-\pi_{\alpha}\rangle|^{2})+\frac{1}{w\tau}O(h^{\infty})e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}}.

(312)

The 1-norm here refers in particular to the integral
on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, i.e.,
\|\cdot\|_{1}=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\cdot|\mathrm{d}x
. We use
\mathrm{d}x

to denote the volume element of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
induced from
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
As
\sin(\cdot)\leq 1
, it is easily estimated from (
312
) that

\displaystyle|\rho_{\rm QTW}-\mu_{\rm QTW}|

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{j(\beta)\neq j(\alpha)}\frac{1}{d|w|\tau}\frac{1}{(2d)^{2}}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}+\sum_{j(\beta)=j(\alpha)}\frac{1}{d|w|\tau}\frac{d-1}{(2d)^{2}}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{|w|\tau}\frac{d-1}{4d}(|\langle x|j(\alpha),\pi_{\alpha}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle x|j(\alpha),-\pi_{\alpha}\rangle|^{2})

\displaystyle\quad+\frac{1}{(d-1)|w|\tau}\frac{1}{4d}(|\langle x|j(\alpha),\pi_{\alpha}\rangle|^{2}+|\langle x|j(\alpha),-\pi_{\alpha}\rangle|^{2})+\frac{1}{|w|\tau}O(h^{\infty})e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}},

(313)

Note that for any
\beta
,
\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}|\langle x|\beta\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}x=1
, so we can obtain

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\rm QTW}-\mu_{\rm QTW}\|_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{|w|\tau}\Big(\frac{d-1}{d^{3}}+\frac{d-1}{2d}+\frac{1}{2d(d-1)}+O(h^{\infty})\Big),

(314)

which gives (
90
).
∎

C.3.3
Proof of
Lemma
3.8

Proof.

\displaystyle p(\infty,\beta|\alpha)

\displaystyle=\sum_{E_{k}=E_{k^{\prime}}}\langle\beta|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|\beta\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}}+\sum_{k=k^{\prime}=d}+\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\{d+1,\ldots,2d\}}\langle\beta|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|\beta\rangle

(315)

Apply the results from
Lemma
3.6
, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}}\langle\beta|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|\beta\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\{1,\ldots,d-1\}}\frac{1}{(2d)^{2}}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{d}[(j(\alpha)-j(\beta))(k^{\prime}-k)]}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{(d-1)^{2}}{(2d)^{2}},\penalty\ j(\alpha)=j(\beta),\\
\frac{1}{(2d)^{2}},\penalty\ j(\alpha)\neq j(\beta),\end{array}\right.

\langle\beta|E_{d}\rangle\langle E_{d}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{d}\rangle\langle E_{d}|\beta\rangle=\frac{1}{(2d)^{2}},

(318)

\displaystyle\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\{d+1,\ldots,2d\}}\langle\beta|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|\alpha\rangle\langle\alpha|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|\beta\rangle

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\{d+1,\ldots,2d\}}\frac{1}{4}\delta_{k-d,j(\alpha)}\delta_{k-d,j(\beta)}\delta_{k^{\prime}-d,j(\alpha)}\delta_{k^{\prime}-d,j(\beta)}

\displaystyle=\sum_{k,k^{\prime}\in\{d+1,\ldots,2d\}}\frac{1}{4}\delta_{k,k^{\prime}}\delta_{j(\alpha),j(\beta)}\delta_{k-d,j(\alpha)}

\displaystyle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{1}{4},\penalty\ j(\alpha)=j(\beta),\\
0,\penalty\ j(\alpha)\neq j(\beta),\end{array}\right.

which complete the proof.
∎

C.3.4
Proof of
Lemma
3.9

The proof of
Lemma
3.9
makes use of
Proposition
A.6
.
So, we should first study the Agmon geodesics and Agmon distances between minima.
The following Lemma and Corollary give the explicit value of the shortest Agmon distance
S_{0}=\min_{\alpha\neq\beta}d(\alpha,\beta)
whose proof indicates there is at most one geodesic with the Agmon length
S_{0}
linking two different minima.

Lemma C.2
.

Constrained on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, for two wells
\alpha\neq\beta
,

d(\pi_{\alpha}a_{j(\alpha)},\pi_{\beta}a_{j(\beta)})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\sqrt{2},\penalty\ j(\alpha)=j(\beta),\\
\sqrt{2}/2,\penalty\ j(\alpha)\neq j(\beta),\end{array}\right.

(321)

where,
d(\cdot,\cdot)
refers to the Agmon distance (see
Definition
2.5
or
Appendix
A.2.2
for more details).

Corollary C.1
.

S_{0}=\sqrt{2}/2
.

Proof of
Lemma
C.2
.

First, we prove that for
j(\alpha)\neq j(\beta)
, there exists an Agmon geodesic linking local minima
\pi_{\alpha}a_{j(\alpha)}
and
\pi_{\beta}a_{j(\beta)}
which is in the plane spanned by

a_{j(\alpha)}
and
a_{j(\beta)}
. Also, this geodesic will also pass through
-\pi_{\alpha}a_{j(\alpha)}
, joining
\pm\pi_{\alpha}a_{j(\alpha)}
.
Because
\{a_{j}\}
symmetrically distribute on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and are orthonormal, the proof can be completed if we prove there exists such a geodesic between
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
.
We work in the coordinate
\psi^{\mu}=\{\psi^{1},\ldots,\psi^{d-1}\}
which can represent
\{x^{1},\ldots,x^{d}\}
, the coordinate in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, as follows:

\displaystyle x^{1}

\displaystyle=\cos\psi^{1}

\displaystyle x^{2}

\displaystyle=\sin\psi^{1}\cos\psi^{2}

\displaystyle x^{3}

\displaystyle=\sin\psi^{1}\sin\psi^{2}\cos\psi^{3}

\displaystyle\cdots

\displaystyle x^{d-1}

\displaystyle=\sin\psi^{1}\sin\psi^{2}\cdots\sin\psi^{d-2}\cos\psi^{d-1}

\displaystyle x^{d}

\displaystyle=\sin\psi^{1}\sin\psi^{2}\cdots\sin\psi^{d-2}\sin\psi^{d-1},

(322)

where,
\psi^{\mu}\in[0,\pi]\times[0,\pi]\times\cdots\times[0,\pi]\times[0,2\pi]
.
The objective function
f(x)=1-\sum_{i}(x^{i})^{4}
can be rewritten as

f(\psi^{\mu})=1-\cos^{4}\psi^{1}-\sin^{4}\psi^{1}\cos^{4}\psi^{2}-\cdots-\sin^{4}\psi^{1}\sin^{4}\psi^{2}\cdots\sin^{4}\psi^{d-2}\sin^{4}\psi^{d-1}.

(323)

The metric of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
, which is induced by the Euclidean metric of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, is given by

dx^{2}=(\mathrm{d}\psi^{1})^{2}+\sin^{2}\psi^{1}(\mathrm{d}\psi^{2})^{2}+\sin^{2}\psi^{1}\sin^{2}\psi^{2}(\mathrm{d}\psi^{3})^{2}+\cdots+\prod_{\nu=1}^{d-2}\sin^{2}\psi^{\nu}(\mathrm{d}\psi^{d-1})^{2},

(324)

or equivalently,

g_{\mu\nu}=\left(\begin{array}[]{ccccc}1&&&&\\
&\sin^{2}\psi^{1}&&&\\
&&\sin^{2}\psi^{1}\sin^{2}\psi^{2}&&\\
&&&\ddots&\\
&&&&\prod_{k=1}^{d-2}\sin^{2}\psi^{k}\\
\end{array}\right)

(325)

with
dx^{2}=g_{\mu\nu}\mathrm{d}\psi^{\mu}\mathrm{d}\psi^{\nu}
.
Through out this proof, we use Einstein’s summation convention.
The Agmon metric is given by
\mathrm{d}s^{2}=V\mathrm{d}x^{2}
, indicating that

ds^{2}=G_{\mu\nu}\mathrm{d}\psi^{\mu}\mathrm{d}\psi^{\nu},\quad G_{\mu\nu}=fg_{\mu\nu}.

(326)

A geodesic is also a curve which can be described by a real parameter (i.e.,
\psi^{\mu}=\psi^{\mu}(\tau)
,
\tau\in\mathbb{R}
).
Geodesics associated to the Agmon metric should satisfy geodesic equations:

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\psi^{\mu}}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}+\Gamma^{\mu}_{\rho\sigma}\frac{\mathrm{d}\psi^{\rho}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\frac{\mathrm{d}\psi^{\sigma}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=0,

(327)

where
\Gamma^{\mu}_{\rho\sigma}
are the Christoffel symbols associated to the Agmon metric.
For
G_{\mu\nu}
, which is a diagonal metric, the Christoffel symbols are given by

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}

\displaystyle=0

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\mu}

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}(G_{\lambda\lambda})^{-1}\partial_{\lambda}G_{\mu\mu}

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\lambda\mu}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}\big(\ln\sqrt{|G_{\lambda\lambda}|}\big)

\displaystyle\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\lambda\lambda}

\displaystyle=\partial_{\lambda}\big(\ln\sqrt{|G_{\lambda\lambda}|}\big),

(328)

where, in these expressions,
\mu\neq\nu\neq\lambda
, and repeated indices are not summed over.
Recall that
\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\mu\nu}=\Gamma^{\lambda}_{\nu\mu}
, all the Christoffel symbols have been given by the upper four identities.
In the plane spanned by
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, we can have
\psi^{k}=0\penalty\ (k\geq 2)
, which satisfies (
327
) for
\mu\geq 2
naturally. And for
\mu=1
, (
327
) reduces to

\frac{\mathrm{d}^{2}\psi^{1}}{\mathrm{d}\tau^{2}}+\frac{2\sin 4\psi^{1}}{1-\cos 4\psi^{1}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}\right)^{2}=0.

(329)

Let
y=\frac{\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}}{\mathrm{d}\tau}
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}y}{\mathrm{d}\tau}=-\frac{2\sin 4\psi^{1}}{1-\cos 4\psi^{1}}y^{2}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\mathrm{d}\left(\frac{1}{y}\right)=\frac{2\sin 4\psi^{1}}{1-\cos 4\psi^{1}}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}}\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}}\right)=\frac{2\sin 4\psi^{1}}{1-\cos 4\psi^{1}}\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\left(\frac{\mathrm{d}\tau}{\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}}\right)\Big/\sqrt{1-\cos 4\psi^{1}}=\rm const.

(330)

Given initial conditions, this ordinary differential equation clearly has a solution. Here, we set
\tau
to be the Agmon length of the curve and
\tau=0
when
\psi^{1}=0
. Then, the solution is
given by

\tau=\int_{0}^{\psi^{1}}\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-\cos 4\psi}\mathrm{d}\psi\equiv\gamma(\psi^{1}).

(331)

So, we prove the existence of a geodesic which can be described by

\psi^{1}=\gamma^{-1}(\tau),\penalty\ \psi^{k}=0\penalty\ (k\geq 2).

(332)

This geodesic associated to the Agmon metric
is the intersection of
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
and the plane spanned by
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, which is an one-dimensional circle, namely, a geodesic associated to the metric
g_{\mu\nu}
(shown in
Figure
12
). We can easily see that the shorter Agmon geodesic linking
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
in the plane spanned by
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
has the Agmon length
\gamma(\pi/2)
. And, there are two paths with identical Agmon length
\gamma(\pi)
joining
a_{1}
and
-a_{1}
which are Agmon geodesics.
The integrals of the Agmon lengths are given by

\displaystyle\gamma(\pi/2)

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-\cos 4\psi}\mathrm{d}\psi

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{\pi/4}\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{1-\cos 4\psi}\mathrm{d}\psi

\displaystyle=2\int_{0}^{2}\frac{1}{8}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2-t}}\mathrm{d}t\quad(\mathrm{let}\penalty\ t=1-\cos 4\psi)

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{2-t}|_{0}^{2}=\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2},

(333)

\gamma(\pi)=2\gamma(\pi/2)=\sqrt{2}.

(334)

Figure 12:
Sketch of the geodesic associated to the Agmon metric in the plane spanned by
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
: red point refers to
a_{1}
, blue point
a_{2}
, green point
-a_{1}
, and the purple point the separating saddle point (see
Definition
A.2
) between
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, respectively.

Recall that
d(\cdot,\cdot)
is the Agmon distance between two points or sets. From now on, we will prove that
for
j(\alpha)\neq j(\beta)
,
d(\pi_{\alpha}a_{j(\alpha)},\pi_{\beta}a_{j(\beta)})=\sqrt{2}/2
, and for any
\alpha
,

d(\pi_{\alpha}a_{j(\alpha)},-\pi_{\alpha}a_{j(\alpha)})=\sqrt{2}
. Because of the landscape symmetry, we only need to
prove that
d(a_{1},a_{2})=\sqrt{2}/2
and
d(a_{1},-a_{1})=\sqrt{2}
, which is equivalent to say that the Agmon geodesic
described by (
332
) with
0\leq\tau\leq\sqrt{2}/2
(
0\leq\tau\leq\sqrt{2}
) is the shortest joining
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
(
a_{1}
and
-a_{1}
).

First note that for
\sum_{i}(x^{i})^{2}=1
or
\sum_{i=2}^{d}(x^{i})^{2}=1-(x^{1})^{2}
, equivalently,

\sum_{i}(x^{i})^{4}=(x^{1})^{4}+\sum_{i=2}^{d}(x^{i})^{4}\leq(x^{1})^{4}+(1-(x^{1})^{2})^{2}.

(335)

Therefore,

f(\psi^{\mu})=f(\psi^{1},\psi^{2},\cdots,\psi^{d})=1-\sum_{i}(x^{i})^{4}\geq 1-(x^{1})^{4}-(1-(x^{1})^{2})^{2}=f(\psi^{1},0,\cdots,0),

(336)

where the coordinates
\psi^{\mu}
and
x^{\nu}
obey the transformation relation (
322
).
For any curve whose parameter is
\tau
, we have

\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{g_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial\psi^{\mu}}{\partial\tau}\frac{\partial\psi^{\nu}}{\partial\tau}}\mathrm{d}\tau.

(337)

And if the parameter
\tau
can be set to be
\psi^{1}
,

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{g_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial\psi^{\mu}}{\partial\psi^{1}}\frac{\partial\psi^{\nu}}{\partial\psi^{1}}}\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}=\sqrt{\sum_{i}g_{ii}\left(\frac{\partial\psi^{i}}{\partial\psi^{1}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}\geq\sqrt{g_{11}\left(\frac{\partial\psi^{1}}{\partial\psi^{1}}\right)^{2}}\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}=\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}.

(338)

For any curve
C
with finite length that can be described by
\psi^{1}
(i.e.,
\psi^{\mu}=\psi^{\mu}(\psi^{1})
and
\psi^{1}\in[a,b]
),
we can conclude that

\int_{C}\sqrt{f}\mathrm{d}x=\int_{a}^{b}\sqrt{fg_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial\psi^{\mu}}{\partial\psi^{1}}\frac{\partial\psi^{\nu}}{\partial\psi^{1}}}\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}\geq\int_{a}^{b}\sqrt{f(\psi^{1},0,\cdots,0)}\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}=\gamma(b)-\gamma(a).

(339)

Let
\psi^{i}=l^{i}(\tau)\penalty\ (0\leq\tau\leq M_{l})
be an arbitrary smooth path joining
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
(
\tau=0
and
\tau=M_{l}
correspond to
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
, respectively). If this path can be described by
\psi^{1}
(i.e.,
\psi^{1}=l^{1}(\tau)
is a bijection and
\psi^{i}=l^{i}((l^{1})^{-1}(\psi^{1}))\penalty\ (0\leq\psi^{1}\leq\pi/2)
), the Agmon length between
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
along this path is given by

l(a_{1},a_{2})\equiv\int_{0}^{M_{l}}\sqrt{fg_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial\psi^{\mu}}{\partial\tau}\frac{\partial\psi^{\nu}}{\partial\tau}}\mathrm{d}\tau=\int_{0}^{\pi/2}\sqrt{fg_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial\psi^{\mu}}{\partial\psi^{1}}\frac{\partial\psi^{\nu}}{\partial\psi^{1}}}\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}\geq\gamma(\pi/2).

(340)

However, if
\psi^{1}=l^{1}(\tau)
is not a bijection, or there is no one-to-one correspondence between
\psi^{1}
and the points in the path, the second equality in the above equation will be invalid.
In this case, we should restrict the domain on
\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{i}[\tau_{i}^{-},\tau_{i}^{+}]\subset[0,M_{l}]
(
\tau_{i}^{+}\leq\tau_{i+1}^{+}
) such that
l^{1}:\mathcal{D}\to[0,\pi/2]
is a bijection and
[0,M_{l}]-\mathcal{D}
is a zero measure set, which can always be done as the path is smooth. Then, we have

\displaystyle l(a_{1},a_{2})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}\int_{\tau_{i}^{-}}^{\tau_{i}^{+}}\sqrt{fg_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial\psi^{\mu}}{\partial\tau}\frac{\partial\psi^{\nu}}{\partial\tau}}\mathrm{d}\tau

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int_{l^{1}(\tau_{i}^{-})}^{l^{1}(\tau_{i}^{+})}\sqrt{fg_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial\psi^{\mu}}{\partial\psi^{1}}\frac{\partial\psi^{\nu}}{\partial\psi^{1}}}\mathrm{d}\psi^{1}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}[\gamma(l^{1}(\tau_{i}^{+}))-\gamma(l^{1}(\tau_{i}^{-}))]=\gamma(\pi/2).

(341)

To sum up, for paths joining
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
,
\gamma(\pi/2)
is the shortest Agmon length, and thus, the Agmon distance between
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
. For the same reason (
339
),
\gamma(\pi)
can be proved to be the Agmon distance between
a_{1}
and
-a_{1}
(more specifically, the proof is the above paragraph with
a_{2}
and
\pi/2
replaced by
-a_{1}
and
\pi
, respectively), which completes the proof of
Lemma
C.2
.
∎

Now, we are able to prove
Lemma
3.9
as follows.

Proof of
Lemma
3.9
.

Because of the symmetry, we can calculate
w
focusing on the interaction between
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
.
The point on the geodesic (see
Figure
12
) separating
the two wells containing
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
should be
x^{\circ}_{12}:=(a_{1}+a_{2})/\sqrt{2}
which is also a saddle point.
Restate
Proposition
A.6
in the present case,
we have

\displaystyle w=-\sqrt{h}(b+O(h))e^{-\frac{S_{0}}{h}},

(342)

where

\displaystyle b=2(2\pi)^{\frac{d-2}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{1/2}{\mathrm{det}^{\prime}\nabla^{2}(d(x^{\circ}_{12},a_{1})+d(x^{\circ}_{12},a_{2}))}}a_{0}^{(1)}(x^{\circ}_{12})a_{0}^{(2)}(x^{\circ}_{12}),

(343)

and
a_{0}^{(1)}(x^{\circ}_{12})=a_{0}^{(2)}(x^{\circ}_{12})
(due to the symmetry) are factions in the WKB estimation.

We first evaluate the function
a^{(1)}_{0}(x)
.
The initial condition (
159
) should be

\displaystyle a^{(1)}_{0}(a_{1})=\left(\frac{\det\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(a_{1})}}{(\sqrt{2}\pi)^{d-1}}\right)^{\frac{1}{4}}.

(344)

The transport equation (
158
) in this case should be

\displaystyle 2\nabla d(x,a_{1})\cdot\nabla a^{(1)}_{0}(x)+(\Delta d(x,a_{1})-\mu)a^{(1)}_{0}(x)=0.

(345)

Note that all the differential operator
\nabla
here adapts to the manifold
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
.
Because
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
can be embedded in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, we may use the differential operator
\nabla_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}
to calculate the Hessian matrix
\nabla^{2}f(a_{1})
:

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}f(a_{1})=\mathbf{P}_{a_{1}^{\perp}}[\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(a_{1})-(\nabla f(a_{1}))^{\top}a_{1}\mathbf{I}]\mathbf{P}_{a_{1}^{\perp}},

(346)

where
\mathbf{I}
is the identity matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
,
\mathbf{P}_{a_{1}^{\perp}}=\mathbf{I}-a_{1}a_{1}^{\top}
is the orthogonal projector onto the tangent space
T_{a_{1}}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,

\nabla^{2}_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}f(a_{1})
is the Hessian matrix in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, and
(\nabla f(a_{1}))^{\top}a_{1}\mathbf{I}
reflects the curvature of the sphere.
Under the coordinate
\{a_{2},a_{3},\ldots,a_{d}\}
which can also be seen
as a local orthonormal coordinate of
T_{a_{1}}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
we can explicitly write
\nabla^{2}f(a_{1})=\mathrm{diag}(4,\ldots 4)
.
So, we have
\det\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(a_{1})}=2^{d-1}
.
To get
x^{\circ}_{12}
, we should use the transport equation.
For any point
x
on the geodesic linking
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
,
in the tangent space
T_{x}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
,
we set
\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{1}}
to be the tangent vector of the geodesic.
Then, because of the symmetry,

\displaystyle\nabla d(x,a_{1})\cdot\nabla a^{(1)}_{0}(x)=\frac{\partial d(x,a_{1})}{\partial x^{1}}\frac{\partial a^{(1)}_{0}(x)}{\partial x^{1}}.

(347)

Similarly,
\Delta d(x,a_{1})
should be of the form

\displaystyle\Delta d(x,a_{1})=f_{1}(x)+(d-2)f_{2}(x),

(348)

for some functions
f_{1}
and
f_{2}
.
For sufficiently small
h
, the energy of the ground state
\mu
should be

\displaystyle\mu=h\mathrm{tr}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(a_{1})}\propto(d-1).

(349)

At last, the transport equation restricted on the geodesic linking
a_{1}
and
a_{2}
can be changed to

\displaystyle\frac{\partial a^{(1)}_{0}(x(\tau))}{\partial\tau}=(d-1)f_{3}(\tau)a^{(1)}_{0}(x(\tau)),

(350)

for some function
f_{3}(\tau)
. Here,
\tau
is the parameter of the geodesic.
In this way, we know

\displaystyle\ln\frac{a^{(1)}_{0}(x^{\circ}_{12})}{a^{(1)}_{0}(a_{1})}\propto d-1,

(351)

and the coefficient is independent of
d
.
As a result, we know that
\ln a^{(1)}_{0}(x^{\circ}_{12})\propto d-1

and the coefficient is independent of
d
.

Note that although
d(x,a_{1})+d(x,a_{2}))

is a complicated function, it is symmetric in the
d-2
-dimension subspace of
T_{x^{\circ}_{12}}\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
which is orthogonal to the tangent vector of the geodesic linking
a_{1}
and
s_{2}
.
Thus, we claim that, similar to the Hessian matrix of
f
,

\nabla^{2}(d(x,a_{1})+d(x,a_{2})))
has
d-2
entries of the same value which are independent of
d
.
This fact give that
\ln\mathrm{det}^{\prime}\nabla^{2}(d(x^{\circ}_{12},a_{1})+d(x^{\circ}_{12},a_{2}))\propto d-2
.

Combining all the results above, we find that
b
is of the form
C_{1}C_{2}^{d-1}
where
C_{1}
and
C_{2}
are two constants independent of
d
.

Given that
S_{0}=\sqrt{2}/2
, we can have

\displaystyle w=-\sqrt{h}(C_{1}C_{2}^{d-1}+O(h))e^{-\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2h}}

(352)

as expected.
∎

C.3.5
Proof of
Proposition
3.1

Proposition
3.1
presents a general upper bound for the total time needed for finding all orthogonal components by QTW.
Constrain on the expected risk,
\mathrm{E}_{x\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}f(x)=\delta
, control the accuracy of QTW and will give
h
.

Proof.

We first estimate the expected risk,
\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}f(x)
.
For sufficiently small
h
,
\mu_{\rm QTW}
localizes near minima.
So, only regions near minima can contribute to the integral

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}f(x)=\int_{\mathbb{S}^{d-1}}f(x)\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\mathrm{d}x.

(353)

Namely,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}f(x)=\sum_{\alpha}\int_{\Omega_{\alpha}}f(x)\mu_{\rm QTW}(x)\mathrm{d}x+O(h^{\infty}),

(354)

where
\Omega_{\alpha}
is a small neighborhood of the minima labeled by
\alpha
.
The term
O(h^{\infty})
appears because
\mu_{\rm QTW}
is a mixing of
|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{\beta}\right\rangle|^{2}
which decay exponentially with respect to
d(x,\beta)/h
(or, apply
Corollary
A.1
).
Still, we choose
h
to be enough small, such that
\Omega_{\alpha}

can be seen as a subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.
Then, with the same Gaussian integral in
Lemma
4.2
,
we have

h=\frac{\delta}{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}\sum_{\beta}p(\infty,\beta|\alpha)\mathrm{tr}\big(\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(\beta)}\big)+o_{\delta}(1)}=\frac{\delta}{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}\mathrm{tr}\big(\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(a_{1})}\big)+o_{\delta}(1)}.

(355)

Here,
\nabla
should still be the differential operator adapted to the sphere manifold and the second equality is valid because
\nabla^{2}f(\beta)
should be the same regardless of
\beta
.
As is calculated in the proof of
Lemma
3.9
,
the matrix form of
\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(a_{1})}
can be
\mathrm{diag}(2,\ldots,2)
. Thus,
\mathrm{tr}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(a_{1})}=2(d-1)
, which gives

\displaystyle h=\frac{\delta}{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{2}(d-1)+o_{\delta}(1)}.

(356)

Substituting this result in
w
and note the relationship between

T_{\rm tot}
and
w
in (
93
), we have

\displaystyle T_{\rm tot}=\tilde{O}(d^{2})\frac{1}{\epsilon(\sqrt{\delta}C_{1}C_{2}^{d-1}+O(\delta))}e^{\frac{d-1+o_{\delta}(1)}{2\delta}}.

(357)

Note that
C_{2}
should be less
1
as the tunneling effect
w
would not increase exponentially with respect to
d
,
for small
\delta
, we can have

\displaystyle T_{\rm tot}=O(\mathrm{poly}(1/\delta,e^{d},1/\epsilon))e^{\frac{d-1+o_{\delta}(1)}{2\delta}},

(358)

which completes the proof.
∎

Appendix D
Technical Details for Quantum-Classical Comparisons

D.1
Details of comparison standards

D.1.1
Proof of
Lemma
4.1

In
Lemma
4.1
, we estimate the expected risk yielded by QTW and SGD for a quadratic function.

Proof.

Firstly, as there is only one (local) minimum,
\mu_{\rm QTW}
is the distribution of the ground state
|E_{0}\rangle
,
namely,

\mu_{\rm QTW}=|\langle x|E_{0}\rangle|^{2}=\frac{\big(\det\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(0)}\big)^{1/2}}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/2}}e^{-\frac{x^{\top}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(0)}x}{\sqrt{2}h}}

(359)

Let
\{x_{i}\}
be a coordinate where
\nabla^{2}f(0)
is a
diagonal matrix denoted by
\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},..,\lambda_{d})
.
For simplicity, set
\omega_{i}=\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}
,

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}f(x)
is then given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}f(x)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{(\prod_{i}\omega_{i})^{1/2}}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/2}}e^{-\frac{\sum_{i}\omega_{i}x_{i}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}h}}\frac{1}{2}\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}x_{i}^{2}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\frac{(\prod_{i}\omega_{i})^{1/2}}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/2}}\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\int e^{-\frac{\sum_{i}\omega_{i}x_{i}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}h}}x_{j}^{2}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}h}{4}\sum_{j}\omega_{j},

(360)

where the last equality uses the facts

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-ax^{2}}\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{a}},

(361)

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^{2}e^{-ax^{2}}\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{a^{3}}}.

(362)

Similarly, we have

\int e^{\frac{-2f}{s}}\mathrm{d}x=\int e^{\frac{-\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}x_{i}^{2}}{s}}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{d}=\frac{(\pi s)^{d/2}}{\sqrt{\prod_{i}\lambda_{i}}},

(363)

and

\int e^{\frac{-2f}{s}}f(x)\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\sum_{j}\lambda_{j}\int e^{\frac{-\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}x_{i}^{2}}{s}}x_{j}^{2}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{d}=\frac{sd}{4}\frac{(\pi s)^{d/2}}{\sqrt{\prod_{i}\lambda_{i}}},

(364)

which complete the proof.
∎

D.1.2
Proof of
Lemma
4.2

With the help of
Lemma
4.1
,
Lemma
4.2
captures
h
and
s
by
Standard
4.1
for general landscapes satisfying assumptions in
Section
2.2
and
Section
2.3
. Note that in
Section
4.1
,
normal lower-case letters,
x
,
y
,…, are used to denote vectors without ambiguity.
However, in the proof, we will use coordinates and components.
Therefore, bold lower-case letters
\mathbf{x}
,
\mathbf{y}
,…, are used to denote vectors or points in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
.

Proof.

For a general landscape
f
satisfying assumptions in
Section
2.2
and
Section
2.3
,
we can find a
sufficiently large but bounded set
\Omega
containing all the
minima
\{\mathbf{x}_{j}:j=1,\ldots,N\}
.
First recall
Lemma
3.2
which gives

\displaystyle\mu_{\rm QTW}(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{j}p(\infty,j)|\langle\mathbf{x}|j\rangle|^{2}+O(h^{\infty}),

(365)

where
p(\infty,j)
is the probability of finding the system at state
\left|j\right\rangle
for
\tau\to\infty
.
Every local ground state decreases exponentially with respect to
1/h

out of
\Omega
, giving that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\Omega}\mu_{\rm QTW}(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=O(h^{\infty}).

(366)

In the region
\Omega
, we first study the integral near one minima
\mathbf{x}_{j}
. Without loss of generality, we can translate the coordinate to
\{x_{k},k=1,\ldots,d\}
such that
\mathbf{x}_{j}=0
. By properly rescaling the coordinate, we can find
a coordinate
\{y_{k}:k=1,\ldots,d\}
and let
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},y_{2},\ldots,y_{d})^{\top}
such that
\sqrt{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{y}}f(0)}=I
where
I

is the
d\times d
identity matrix.
Under the coordinate
\{y_{k}:k=1,\ldots,d\}
, we can always find a fixed ball
\Omega_{j}
only containing one minimum
\mathbf{x}_{j}=0
and for any
\mathbf{y}\in\Omega_{j}
,
\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{y}}f(\mathbf{y})>0
(because
f
is smooth).
The local ground state satisfies

|\langle\mathbf{y}|j\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/2}}(1+O(\|\mathbf{y}\|))e^{-\frac{\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathbf{y}+O(\|\mathbf{y}\|^{3})}{\sqrt{2}h}},

(367)

by WKB approximation, and the function can be rewritten as

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{y})=\frac{1}{2}\mathbf{y}^{\top}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(0)}\mathbf{y}+O(\|\mathbf{y}\|^{3}).

(368)

Then, we define a hypercube
\Omega^{(h)}_{j}
whose edge length (under the coordinate of
\mathbf{y}
) is
\Theta(h^{5/12})
.
Evaluate the integral:

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega^{(h)}_{j}}f(\mathbf{x})|\langle\mathbf{x}|j\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{\Omega^{(h)}_{j}}f(\mathbf{y})|\langle\mathbf{y}|j\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

(369)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega^{(h)}_{j}}\frac{1+O(h^{5/12})}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/2}}e^{-\frac{\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathbf{y}}{\sqrt{2}h}}(1+O(h^{1/4}))(\mathbf{y}^{\top}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(0)}\mathbf{y}+O(h^{5/4}))\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}

(370)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega^{(h)}_{j}}\frac{1}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/2}}e^{-\frac{\mathbf{y}^{\top}\mathbf{y}}{\sqrt{2}h}}(\mathbf{y}^{\top}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(0)}\mathbf{y})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{y}+o(h).

(371)

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{2}\int_{\Omega^{(h)}_{j}}\frac{\big(\det\sqrt{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(0)}\big)^{1/2}}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/2}}e^{-\frac{\mathbf{x}^{\top}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(0)}\mathbf{x}}{\sqrt{2}h}}(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(0)\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}+o(h)

(372)

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}h}{4}\mathrm{tr}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(\mathbf{x}_{j})}+o(h).

(373)

The last equality is valid because
h^{5/12}\gg\sqrt{h}
for small
h
and
\sqrt{h}
is the scale of the standard deviation of the Gaussian distribution.
Calculations of the last integral in can be seen in the proof of
Lemma
4.1
.
Define
f_{j}=\max_{\mathbf{x}\in\Omega_{j}}f(\mathbf{x})
,
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{j}\backslash\Omega^{(h)}_{j}}f(\mathbf{x})|\langle\mathbf{x}|j\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq\int_{\Omega_{j}\backslash\Omega^{(h)}_{j}}f_{j}|\langle\mathbf{x}|j\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=O(h^{\infty}).

(374)

The last equality relies on two facts:
\Omega_{j}
is a bounded region and
\sqrt{h}=o(h^{5/12})
. The first fact ensures that the difference between
|\langle\mathbf{x}|j\rangle|^{2}
and the Gaussian distribution can be bounded by multiplying some constant, and the second fact make the integral of the Gaussian distribution be of
O(h^{\infty})
.
We now have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{j}}f(\mathbf{x})|\langle\mathbf{x}|j\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=\frac{\sqrt{2}h}{4}\mathrm{tr}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(\mathbf{x}_{j})}+o(h).

(375)

In the region
\Omega\backslash\Omega_{j}
,

f
is bounded and by
Corollary
A.1
, the integral of distributions of local ground states should be of
O(h^{\infty})
.
Therefore, we claim that
\int_{\Omega\backslash\Omega_{j}}\mu_{\rm QTW}f\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=O(h^{\infty})
.

Combine all the results above, we can have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}f(\mathbf{x})=\sum_{j=1}^{N}\frac{\sqrt{2}h}{4}\mathrm{tr}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(\mathbf{x}_{j})}+o(h).

(376)

By setting
\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}f(\mathbf{x})=\delta
,

\displaystyle h=\frac{\delta}{\frac{\sqrt{2}}{4}\sum_{j=1}^{N}p(\infty,j)\mathrm{tr}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(\mathbf{x}_{j})}+o_{h}(1)}.

(377)

And because for sufficiently small
h
, we have
h\propto\delta
from the above equation, the term
o_{h}(1)
in the above equation can be replaced by
o_{\delta}(1)
, giving (
100
).

For
\mu_{\rm SGD}
, we still begin with a large region
\Omega
containing all the minima
\{\mathbf{x}_{j}:j=1,\ldots,N\}
.
Note that

\displaystyle\mu_{\rm SGD}(x)=\frac{e^{-\frac{2f(\mathbf{x})}{s}}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2f(\mathbf{x})}{s}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}},

(378)

we first evaluate

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\Omega}\mu_{\rm SGD}(\mathbf{x})f(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\Omega}e^{-\frac{2f(\mathbf{x})}{s}}f(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2f(\mathbf{x})}{s}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}.

(379)

Without loss of generality, we set
f(\partial\Omega)=\{C\}
and
f(\mathbf{x})\leq C
for
\mathbf{x}\in\Omega
.
Define
g=\max(f-C,0)
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\Omega}e^{-\frac{2f(\mathbf{x})}{s}}f(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2f(\mathbf{x})}{s}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}

\displaystyle=\frac{e^{-\frac{2C}{s}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\Omega}e^{-\frac{2g(\mathbf{x})}{s}}g(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}{e^{-\frac{2C}{s}}\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2(f(\mathbf{x})-C)}{s}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}

(380)

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2g(\mathbf{x})}{s}}g(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2g}{s}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\Omega}(e^{-\frac{2(f(\mathbf{x})-C)}{s}}-1)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}.

(381)

Note that
