I_{g}(s):=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2g(\mathbf{x})}{s}}g(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}
decreases when
s
decreases.
Let
\Omega^{\prime}:=\{\mathbf{x}\mid f(\mathbf{x})\leq C/2\}
, we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}(e^{-\frac{2(f(\mathbf{x})-C)}{s}}-1)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}(e^{-\frac{2(f(\mathbf{x})-C)}{s}}-1)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

(382)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{\Omega^{\prime}}(e^{\frac{C}{s}}-1)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=(e^{\frac{C}{s}}-1)\mathrm{Volume}(\Omega^{\prime}).

(383)

Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2g(\mathbf{x})}{s}}g(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2g}{s}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}+\int_{\Omega}(e^{-\frac{2(f(\mathbf{x})-C)}{s}}-1)\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}\leq\frac{I_{g}(s)}{(e^{\frac{C}{s}}-1)\mathrm{Volume}(\Omega^{\prime})}=O(s^{\infty}).

(384)

That is,

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\Omega}\mu_{\rm SGD}(\mathbf{x})f(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=O(s^{\infty}).

(385)

Now, we consider the small neighborhood
\Omega_{j}
only containing one minimum
\mathbf{x}_{j}
. Similar to the quantum case, we let
\mathbf{x}_{j}=0
and introduce a rescaled coordinate
\{y_{k},k=1,\ldots,d\}
and
\mathbf{y}=(y_{1},\ldots,y_{d})^{\top}
such that
\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{y}}f(0)=I
.
Also define the hypercube
\Omega^{s}_{j}
with edge length (under the coordinate of
\mathbf{y}
)
\Theta(s^{5/12})
,
apply the same argument as the quantum case,
we have

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{j}^{s}}e^{-\frac{2f}{s}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=\frac{(\pi s)^{d/2}}{\sqrt{\det\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(0)}}(1+O(s^{1/4}))=\frac{(\pi s)^{d/2}}{\sqrt{\det\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(0)}}(1+o_{s}(1)),

(386)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{j}^{s}}e^{-\frac{2f}{s}}f\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=\frac{sd}{4}\frac{(\pi s)^{d/2}}{\sqrt{\det\nabla^{2}_{\mathbf{x}}f(0)}}(1+o_{s}(1)),

(387)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{j}\backslash\Omega_{j}^{s}}e^{-\frac{2f}{s}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=O(s^{\infty})

(388)

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega_{j}\backslash\Omega_{j}^{s}}e^{-\frac{2f}{s}}f\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=O(s^{\infty}).

(389)

In the region
\Omega\backslash(\bigcup_{j=1}^{N}\Omega_{j})
,
f
is bounded from below and both the integrals of
e^{-\frac{2f}{s}}
and
e^{-\frac{2f}{s}}f
should be of
O(s^{\infty})
.

Finally, we can obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathbf{x}\sim\mu_{\rm SGD}}f(\mathbf{x})=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}\mu_{\rm SGD}(\mathbf{x})f(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\frac{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2f(\mathbf{x})}{s}}f(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}{\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}}e^{-\frac{2f(\mathbf{x})}{s}}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}}=\frac{\frac{sd}{4}(1+o_{s}(1))}{1+O(s^{\infty})}+O(s^{\infty})

\displaystyle=\frac{sd}{4}(1+o_{s}(1)),

(390)

and then

\displaystyle s=\frac{\delta}{\frac{d}{4}(1+o_{s}(1))}.

(391)

Since
s\propto\delta
for small
\delta
or
s
, we can replace
o_{s}(1)
in the above equation to
o_{\delta}(1)
.

The two equations in
Lemma
4.2
are obtained finishing the proof.
∎

D.1.3
Proof of
Lemma
4.3

We estimate the expected distances from the minimum and establish an asymptotic relation between
h
and
s
by
Standard
4.2
in
Lemma
4.3
.

Proof.

Note that

\mu_{\rm QTW}=\frac{\big(\det\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(0)}\big)^{1/2}}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/2}}e^{-\frac{x^{\top}\sqrt{\nabla^{2}f(0)}x}{\sqrt{2}h}}

(392)

We choose
\{x_{i}\}
be a coordinate where
\nabla^{2}f(0)
is a
diagonal matrix denoted by
\mathrm{diag}(\lambda_{1},..,\lambda_{d})
.
Define
\omega_{i}=\sqrt{\lambda_{i}}
,

\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}D(x,0)
is then given by

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{x\sim\mu_{\rm QTW}}D(x,0)

\displaystyle=\int\frac{(\prod_{i}\omega_{i})^{1/2}}{(\sqrt{2}\pi h)^{d/2}}e^{-\frac{\sum_{i}\omega_{i}x_{i}^{2}}{\sqrt{2}h}}\sum_{i}x_{i}^{2}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{d}

\displaystyle=\frac{\sqrt{2}h}{2}\sum_{j}\frac{1}{\omega_{j}},

(393)

where the last equality uses the facts

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}e^{-ax^{2}}\mathrm{d}x=\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{a}},

(394)

\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}x^{2}e^{-ax^{2}}\mathrm{d}x=\frac{1}{2}\sqrt{\frac{\pi}{a^{3}}}.

(395)

Still use the above two Gaussian integrals, we have

\int e^{\frac{-2f}{s}}\mathrm{d}x=\int e^{\frac{-\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}x_{i}^{2}}{s}}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{d}=\frac{(\pi s)^{d/2}}{\sqrt{\prod_{i}\lambda_{i}}},

(396)

and

\int e^{\frac{-2f}{s}}D(x,0)\mathrm{d}x=\sum_{j}\int e^{\frac{-\sum_{i}\lambda_{i}x_{i}^{2}}{s}}x_{j}^{2}\mathrm{d}x_{1}\cdots\mathrm{d}x_{d}=\frac{s}{2}\frac{(\pi s)^{d/2}}{\sqrt{\prod_{i}\lambda_{i}}}\sum_{i}\frac{1}{\lambda_{i}},

(397)

which can readily give
Lemma
4.3
.
∎

D.2
Details about the exponential quantum speedup

D.2.1
Proof of
Lemma
4.12

Proof.

As shown in
Figure
7
, we use
\mathrm{Cone}(\epsilon)
to denote the spherical cone subtended at one end by
\mathrm{Cap}(\epsilon)
. Let
\mathbb{B}^{d}
be the
d
-dimensional unit ball,
\mathrm{Cone}(\epsilon)
can be enclosed in a ball with radius
\sqrt{1-\epsilon^{2}}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Area}(\mathrm{Cap}(\epsilon))}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}=\frac{\mathrm{Volume}(\mathrm{Cone}(\epsilon))}{\mathrm{Volume}(\mathbb{B}^{d})}\leq\frac{\mathrm{Volume}(\sqrt{1-\epsilon^{2}}\mathbb{B}^{d})}{\mathrm{Volume}(\mathbb{B}^{d})}=(1-\epsilon^{2})^{d/2}\leq e^{-d\epsilon^{2}/2}.

(398)

∎

D.2.2
Proof of
Lemma
4.13

Proof.

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{x}\notin S_{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle=P(\mathbf{x}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{0},R),\penalty\ |\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{v}|\leq w)

(399)

\displaystyle=P(\mathbf{y}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{0},1),\penalty\ |\mathbf{y}\cdot\mathbf{v}|\leq w/R)\quad\mathrm{where}\penalty\ \mathbf{y}=\mathbf{x}/R

(400)

\displaystyle\leq P(\mathbf{z}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1},\penalty\ |\mathbf{z}\cdot\mathbf{v}|\leq w/R)

(401)

\displaystyle=2\frac{\mathrm{Area}(\mathrm{Cap}(w/R))}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\leq 2e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}.

(402)

∎

D.2.3
Proof of
Lemma
4.14

Proof.

For the algorithm, the point
\mathbf{x}_{i}
it queried is fixed while
the direction
\mathbf{v}
is unknown. Define
C_{i}=\{\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}:|\mathbf{x}_{i}\cdot\mathbf{v}|>w\}
, we have
\mathbf{v}\notin C_{i}\iff\mathbf{x}_{i}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
. Similar to
Lemma
4.13
,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Area}(C_{i})}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}=2\frac{\mathrm{Area}(\mathrm{Cap}(w/\|\mathbf{x}_{1}\|))}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\leq 2\frac{\mathrm{Area}(\mathrm{Cap}(w/R))}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\leq 2e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}.

(403)

Now, we need to relate the area ratios to the probability we want to calculate. Firstly, examine the term
P(\mathbf{x}_{t}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\forall\tau<t:\mathbf{x}_{\tau}\in S_{\mathbf{v}})
. Given that
\{\forall\tau<t:\mathbf{x}_{\tau}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\}
, we know
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}-\cup_{i=1}^{t-1}C_{i}
. Because
\forall\tau<t,\penalty\ q(\mathbf{x}_{\tau})
is independent of
\mathbf{v}
conditioned on
\{\forall\tau<t:\mathbf{x}_{\tau}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\}
, we have no information about
\mathbf{v}
except
\mathbf{v}\in\mathbb{S}^{d-1}-\cup_{i=1}^{t-1}C_{i}
when determining
\mathbf{x}_{t}
. In this case,
\mathbf{v}
should be uniformly
distributed over
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}-\cup_{i=1}^{t-1}C_{i}
, and we have

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{x}_{t}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\forall\tau<t:\mathbf{x}_{\tau}\in S_{\mathbf{v}})=\frac{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}-\cup_{i=1}^{t}C_{i})}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}-\cup_{i=1}^{t-1}C_{i})}.

(404)

By the product rule, we can get

\displaystyle P(\forall t\leq T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\in S_{\mathbf{v}})=\prod_{t=1}^{T}P(\mathbf{x}_{t}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\forall\tau<t:\mathbf{x}_{\tau}\in S_{\mathbf{v}})=\frac{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}-\cup_{i=1}^{T}C_{i})}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})},

(405)

which leads to

\displaystyle P(\exists t\leq T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin S_{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle=1-P(\forall t\leq T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\in S_{\mathbf{v}})

(406)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{Area}(\cup_{i=1}^{T}C_{i})}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\leq\sum_{i}\frac{\mathrm{Area}(C_{i})}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})}\leq 2Te^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}.

(407)

∎

D.2.4
Proof of
Proposition
4.1

Proof.

On the condition that
\mathbf{x}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}
,

\displaystyle f(\mathbf{x})=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\frac{1}{2}\omega^{2}\|x\|^{2},\penalty\ \|x\|\leq a\\[3.0pt]
H_{2},\penalty\ \|x\|>a,\end{array}\right.

which is independent of
\mathbf{v}
. Any local query
q(\mathbf{x})
is then independent of
\mathbf{v}
.

By
Lemma
4.14
, restricted in
\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{0},R)
, for any classical algorithm with only
T=o(e^{\frac{dw^{2}}{4R^{2}}})
local queries,

\displaystyle P(\exists t\leq T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin S_{\mathbf{v}})\leq 2Te^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}\leq e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{4R^{2}}}.

(410)

Because
W_{+}\cap S_{\mathbf{v}}=\varnothing
, with high probability, namely,
1-e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{4R^{2}}}
, any classical algorithm cannot land in
W_{+}
.

Next, we consider
Problem
4.1
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Let
F=\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{0},R)
and
U_{\mathbf{v}}=\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{0},R)\backslash S_{\mathbf{v}}
.
The probabilities
P(\mathbf{x}_{1}\in U_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\mathbf{x}_{1}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{0},R))\leq 2e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}
and
P(\mathbf{x}_{1}\in U_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\mathbf{x}_{1}\in F)=0
, such
that
P(\forall t\leq T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})\geq 1-2Te^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}
is valid for
T=1
.
Assume that
P(\forall t\leq T-1:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})\geq 1-2(T-1)e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}
is true. We should examine the term
P(\mathbf{x}_{T}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\forall t<T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})
.
Given that
\{\mathbf{x}_{T}\in F\}
,
P(\mathbf{x}_{T}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\mathbf{x}_{T}\in F,\penalty\ \forall t<T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})=1
.
And we have

\displaystyle P(\mathbf{x}_{T}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\forall t<T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})\geq P(\mathbf{x}_{T}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\mathbf{x}_{T}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{0},R),\penalty\ \forall t<T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}}).

(411)

In the queried
T-1
pints, assume that there are
T_{1}-1
points in
S_{\mathbf{v}}
.
All other
T-T_{1}
points and queries in
F
provide no information inside
\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{0},R)
and are independent of
\mathbf{v}
. Therefore,

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69054ptP(\mathbf{x}_{T}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\mathbf{x}_{T}\in\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{0},R),\penalty\ \forall t<T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})

\displaystyle=P(\mathbf{x}_{T_{1}}\in S_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\forall t<T_{1}:\mathbf{x}_{t}\in S_{\mathbf{v}})

(412)

\displaystyle=\frac{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}-\cup_{i=1}^{T_{1}}C_{i})}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}-\cup_{i=1}^{T_{1}-1}C_{i})}\geq 1-\frac{\mathrm{Area}(C_{T_{1}})}{\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1}-\cup_{i=1}^{T_{1}-1}C_{i})}

(413)

\displaystyle\geq 1-\frac{2e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}}{1-2(T_{1}-1)e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}}\geq 1-\frac{2e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}}{1-2(T-1)e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}}.

(414)

In the second equality, we use (
404
) as queries in
S_{\mathbf{v}}
are independent of
\mathbf{v}
. The second inequality is deduced from (
403
).
Finally, we have

\displaystyle P(\forall t\leq T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})=P(\mathbf{x}_{T}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}}\mid\forall t<T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})P(\forall t\leq T-1:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})\geq 1-2Te^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}.

(415)

By mathematical induction,
P(\forall t\leq T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\notin U_{\mathbf{v}})\geq 1-2Te^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}
is true for any
T\in\mathbb{N}_{+}
. In
\mathbb{R}^{d}
, for any classical algorithm given only
T=o(e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{4R^{2}}})
queries, the probability of finding any point in
W_{+}
is given by

\displaystyle P(\exists t\leq T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\in W_{+})\leq P(\exists t\leq T:\mathbf{x}_{t}\in U_{\mathbf{v}})\leq 2Te^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}\leq e^{-\frac{dw^{2}}{4R^{2}}},

(416)

which completes the proof.
∎

D.2.5
Proof of
Lemma
4.15

Proof.

First, the integral in (
131
) can be easily verified. We need to prove that
\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\omega a^{2}+2(b-a)\sqrt{H_{1}}
is the shortest Agmon length of paths linking
U_{-}
and
U_{+}
.

Without loss of generality, we choose an orthonormal basis
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{d}\}
, and write any vector
\mathbf{x}=\sum_{i}x^{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
We set
\mathbf{e}_{1}=\mathbf{v}
.
Let
x^{i}=l^{i}(\tau)\penalty\ (0\leq\tau\leq M_{l})
be an arbitrary smooth path joining
U_{-}
and
U_{+}
, where
\tau
is the parameter of the curve,
l^{i}(0)=0
, and
l^{i}(M_{l})=\delta_{i1}2b
.
If
x^{1}
can be the parameter of this path (i.e.,
x^{1}=l^{1}(\tau)
is a bijection and
x^{i}=l^{i}((l^{1})^{-1}(x^{1}))\penalty\ (0\leq x^{1}\leq 2b)
), the Agmon length between
U_{-}
and
U_{+}
along this path is given by

\displaystyle l(U_{-},U_{+})

\displaystyle=\int_{0}^{M_{l}}\sqrt{f\delta_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial x^{\mu}}{\partial\tau}\frac{\partial x^{\nu}}{\partial\tau}}\mathrm{d}\tau=\int_{0}^{2b}\sqrt{f\delta_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial x^{\mu}}{\partial x^{1}}\frac{\partial x^{\nu}}{\partial x^{1}}}\mathrm{d}x^{1}

(417)

\displaystyle\geq\int_{0}^{2b}\sqrt{f(x^{1}\mathbf{e}_{1})}\mathrm{d}x^{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\omega a^{2}+2(b-a)\sqrt{H_{1}}.

(418)

If
\psi^{1}=l^{1}(\tau)
is not a bijection the second equality in the above equation will be invalid.
In this case, we should restrict the domain on
\mathcal{D}=\bigcup_{i}[\tau_{i}^{-},\tau_{i}^{+}]\subset[0,M_{l}]
(
\tau_{i}^{+}\leq\tau_{i+1}^{-}
) s.t.
l^{1}:\mathcal{D}\to[0,2b]
is a bijection and
[0,M_{l}]-\mathcal{D}
is a zero measure set. Then, we have

\displaystyle l(U_{-},U_{+})

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}\int_{\tau_{i}^{-}}^{\tau_{i}^{+}}\sqrt{f\delta_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial x^{\mu}}{\partial\tau}\frac{\partial x^{\nu}}{\partial\tau}}\mathrm{d}\tau

(419)

\displaystyle=\sum_{i}\int_{l^{1}(\tau_{i}^{-})}^{l^{1}(\tau_{i}^{+})}\sqrt{f\delta_{\mu\nu}\frac{\partial x^{\mu}}{\partial x^{1}}\frac{\partial x^{\nu}}{\partial x^{1}}}\mathrm{d}x^{1}

(420)

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i}\int_{l^{1}(\tau_{i}^{-})}^{l^{1}(\tau_{i}^{+})}\sqrt{f(x^{1}\mathbf{e}_{1})}\mathrm{d}x^{1}=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}\omega a^{2}+2(b-a)\sqrt{H_{1}}.

(421)

This implies the correctness of (
131
).

Next, observe that
(\partial x^{i}/\partial x^{1})^{2}>0
, if any path
x^{i}=l^{i}(\tau)\penalty\ (0\leq\tau\leq M_{l})
is not the one given by (
130
), inequalities in (
418
) and (
421
) will be strict. Therefore, we prove that (
130
) is the only geodesic linking
U_{-}
and
U_{+}
with the Agmon length
S_{0}
.
∎

D.2.6
Proof of
Lemma
4.16

Proof.

Because of the symmetry of the two local ground states,
we can naturally have (
132
).
Since the constructed landscape satisfies assumptions needed for

Proposition
A.6
, we can use
Proposition
A.6
to calculate the tunneling amplitude

\nu
directly. By the geometrical symmetry of the two wells,
the special point on the geodesic
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-+}
should be

b\mathbf{v}
.
The next-to-leading term of
\nu
is then given by

\displaystyle\nu=-h^{1/2}2(2\pi)^{\frac{d-1}{2}}\sqrt{\frac{f(b\mathbf{v})}{\mathrm{det}^{\prime}\Big(\nabla^{2}d_{-+}(b\mathbf{v})\Big)}}a^{(-)}_{0}(b\mathbf{v})a^{(+)}_{0}(b\mathbf{v})e^{-S_{0}/h},

(422)

where
\mathrm{det}^{\prime}
denotes the usual determinant with the
zero mode removed, and
a^{(\pm)}_{0}
are the leading terms in the WKB approximations of the local ground states.

Choose an orthonormal basis
\{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{d}\}
in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
where
\mathbf{e}_{1}=\mathbf{v}
, we have
\mathbf{x}=\sum_{i=1}^{d}x_{i}\mathbf{e}_{i}
.
Since along the geodesic
\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-+}
,
d_{-+}\equiv S_{0}
,
we obtain that
\partial d_{-+}/\partial x_{1}=0
and
\partial^{2}d_{-+}/\partial x_{1}^{2}=0
. That is,
\forall j,\penalty\ (\nabla^{2}d_{-+}(b\mathbf{v}))_{ij}=0
.
Constrained on the hypersurface perpendicular to
\mathbf{e}_{1}
, consider an infinitesimal
\delta x=\sum_{i=2}^{d}\delta x_{i}
and we have

\displaystyle d(b\mathbf{v}+\delta\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0})-d(b\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0})=\frac{\|\delta x\|^{2}}{b}\sqrt{H_{1}}+o(\|\delta x\|^{2}).

(423)

Given that

\displaystyle d(b\mathbf{v}+\delta\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0})-d(b\mathbf{v},\mathbf{0})=\sum_{i,j=2}^{d}\left.\frac{\partial^{2}d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0})}{\partial x_{i}\partial x_{j}}\right|_{\mathbf{x}=b\mathbf{v}}\delta x_{i}\delta x_{j},

(424)

it is readily to get
(\nabla^{2}d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0})|_{\mathbf{x}=b\mathbf{v}})_{i,j}=\delta_{ij}2\sqrt{H_{1}}/b\penalty\ (i,j\geq 2)
.
Similarly, by symmetry, we can obtain
(\nabla^{2}d(\mathbf{x},2b\mathbf{v})|_{\mathbf{x}=b\mathbf{v}})_{i,j}=\delta_{ij}2\sqrt{H_{1}}/b\penalty\ (i,j\geq 2)
.
Use the fact
d_{-+}(\mathbf{x})=d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0})+d(\mathbf{x},2b\mathbf{v})
,
we deduce the following

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}d_{-+}(b\mathbf{v})=\mathrm{diag}\left(0,\frac{4\sqrt{H_{1}}}{b},\ldots,\frac{4\sqrt{H_{1}}}{b}\right)\penalty\ \mathrm{under\penalty\ the\penalty\ basis}\penalty\ \{\mathbf{e}_{1},\ldots,\mathbf{e}_{d}\},

(425)

which leads to

\displaystyle\mathrm{det}^{\prime}\big(\nabla^{2}d_{-+}(b\mathbf{v})\big)=(4\sqrt{H_{1}}/b)^{d-1}.

(426)

Next, we need to calculate
a^{(\pm)}_{0}(b\mathbf{v})
. Using the symmetry between the two local states, we know
a^{(-)}_{0}(b\mathbf{v})=a^{(+)}_{0}(b\mathbf{v})
.
So, we can only focus on the calculation of
a^{(-)}_{0}(\mathbf{x})
, which is the leading term in WKB approximation of the local ground state of the well
U_{-}
.
By
Lemma
A.1
,
a^{(-)}_{0}(\mathbf{x})
should satisfy the first transport equation

\displaystyle 2\nabla d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0})\cdot\nabla a^{(-)}_{0}+(\Delta d(\mathbf{x},\mathbf{0})-E_{1})a^{(-)}_{0}=0,

(427)

where
E_{1}=\omega d/\sqrt{2}
as the neighborhood of the well is quadratic. On any point
\mathbf{x}=x_{1}\mathbf{v}\in\boldsymbol{\gamma}_{-+}
, the transport equation is reduced to

\displaystyle\nabla a^{(-)}_{0}=0,\quad x_{1}\leq a,

(428)

\displaystyle\frac{\partial a^{(-)}_{0}}{\partial x_{1}}+d\left(\frac{d-1}{x_{1}d}-\frac{\omega}{2\sqrt{2H_{1}}}\right)a^{(-)}_{0}=0,\quad a<x_{1}\leq b.

(429)

With the initial condition
a^{(-)}_{0}(\mathbf{0})=(\frac{\omega}{\sqrt{2}\pi})^{d/4}
,
a^{(-)}_{0}(b\mathbf{v})
is determined as

\displaystyle a^{(-)}_{0}(b\mathbf{v})=(\frac{\omega}{\sqrt{2}\pi})^{d/4}\exp\left(\frac{\omega d(b-a)}{2\sqrt{2H_{1}}}-d\ln\frac{b}{a}\right).

(430)

Substitute the results (
426
) and (
430
) into (
422
), we can get (
133
), which finished the proof.
∎

D.2.7
Proof of
Proposition
4.2

Proof.

First of all,
H_{2}
is a constant independent of
d
.
We set
h
to be of the form
h=\frac{4\delta}{\sqrt{2}\omega d}
and
\delta\in(0,\delta_{0}]
is bounded where
\delta_{0}=O(H_{0})
.
To understand this setting, we evaluate the energy of the local ground state
\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle
corresponding to the well
U_{-}
.
Recall that
H_{0}\ll H_{2}
, if
\delta
is small enough to let

\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle
localize in the quadratic region
W_{-}
,
the energy of
\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle
should be approximately
h\mathrm{tr}\nabla^{2}f(\mathbf{0})/\sqrt{2}=hd\omega/\sqrt{2}=2\delta\ll H_{2}
.
If
\delta
is of the order
H_{0}
, a conservative estimation is that
the potential energy
\left\langle\Phi_{-}\right|f\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle=O(H_{0})
and the
kinetic energy is
\left\langle\Phi_{-}\right|-h^{2}\Delta\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle=O(dh^{2}(\frac{2\pi}{D})^{2})
where
D=\Omega(a)
can be seen as the
diameter of the region where
\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle
is localized.
Therefore, the energy of
\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle
can be always far less than
H_{2}
, and
\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle
should be localized in the region with
low potential energy
W_{-}\cup B_{\mathbf{v}}\cup W_{+}
.
A more quantitative way of seeing
h=\frac{4\delta}{\sqrt{2}\omega d}
is that this setting solves the problem associated to measure concentration.

Together with
Lemma
4.15
and
Lemma
4.16
,
we have

\displaystyle\nu=-\sqrt{\frac{8hH_{1}\sqrt{H_{1}}}{\pi b}}\exp\left(\Gamma\Big(\frac{H_{0}}{\delta},\frac{b}{a},\frac{H_{0}}{H_{1}}\Big)d\right),

(431)

where

\displaystyle\Gamma(x,y,z):=-\frac{1}{2}x+(y-1)\sqrt{z}\big(1-\frac{x}{z}\big)-\frac{3}{2}\ln y-\ln\sqrt{2}+\frac{1}{4}\ln z.

(432)

Let
\left|\Phi_{+}\right\rangle
be the local ground state corresponding to
U_{+}
.
The key to make our analysis on
\nu
valid is that orthonormalized local ground states
\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle
and
\left|\Phi_{+}\right\rangle
span the 2-dimensional subspace spanned by
\left|E_{0}\right\rangle
and
\left|E_{1}\right\rangle
. Here,
\left|E_{0}\right\rangle
and
\left|E_{1}\right\rangle
are the ground state and the first excited state, respectively.
To be more specific,
let
E_{0}
and
E_{1}
be the energy of
\left|E_{0}\right\rangle
and
\left|E_{1}\right\rangle
, respectively, then

\displaystyle\left|E_{0}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle+\left|\Phi_{+}\right\rangle),\quad\left|E_{1}\right\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{2}}(\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle-\left|\Phi_{+}\right\rangle),

(433)

and

\displaystyle E_{0}=\mu+\nu,\quad E_{1}=\mu-\nu,

(434)

where
\mu
is the energy of
\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle
(or
\left|\Phi_{+}\right\rangle
) and without loss of generality, inner products
\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{-}}\right\rangle
and
\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle
are real and positive.
Only studying
\nu
, it seems we can let
|\nu|
increase exponentially with respect to
d
, but this is infeasible in certain.
To make this scenario valid, we should have
|\nu|\ll\mu
and
|\nu|\ll E_{2}-E_{1}
, where
E_{2}
is the energy of the second excited state.
Next, we show these constraints can be satisfied while
|\nu|
is not too small.
Let
\mu^{\prime}
be the energy of the local first excited states,
17
17

17

In general, there may be many local eigenstates with the same energy
\mu^{\prime}
. This phenomenon is called the degeneracy of energy levels.
we set
|\nu^{\prime}|=\mu^{\prime}-E_{2}
.
Then,
|\nu|\ll E_{2}-E_{1}
is equivalent to
|\nu|+|\nu^{\prime}|\ll\mu^{\prime}-\mu
.
Since
g_{1}:=\mu^{\prime}-\mu
is the energy gap between the local ground state and local first excited states which all concentrate near the region
W_{-}
(or
W_{+}
),
18
18

18

Because of the symmetry of
W_{-}
and
W_{+}
, it suffices to study one region
W_{-}
and the conclusion is also true for
W_{+}
.

its value satisfies
g_{1}=\Omega(h^{2})=\Omega(1/\mathrm{poly}(d))

Andrews and Clutterbuck 2011
.
Let
g_{2}=1/\mathrm{poly}(d)
be a function of
d
such that
g_{2}=o_{d}(g_{1})
, we further demand that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{d}}\exp\left(\Gamma\Big(\frac{H_{0}}{\delta},\frac{b}{a},\frac{H_{0}}{H_{1}}\Big)d\right)=g_{2}(d),

(435)

which gives

\displaystyle\Gamma\Big(\frac{H_{0}}{\delta},\frac{b}{a},\frac{H_{0}}{H_{1}}\Big)=-\frac{1}{d}\ln\mathrm{poly}(d)\to 0,\penalty\ (d\to\infty).

(436)

Eq. (
436
) can always be valid by properly choosing the parameters
(\frac{H_{0}}{\delta},\frac{b}{a},\frac{H_{0}}{H_{1}})
.
If we demand
H_{1}
and
b
to be two constants independent of
d
,

(\frac{H_{0}}{\delta},\frac{b}{a},\frac{H_{0}}{H_{1}})
are still three free variables.
Adding the restriction (
436
), there are two free variables in
(\frac{H_{0}}{\delta},\frac{b}{a},\frac{H_{0}}{H_{1}})
left and
we can obtain
|\nu|=o_{d}(g_{1})
.
Although
|\nu^{\prime}|
which captures tunneling effects between
local excited states is difficult to be calculated explicitly,
we claim that
|\nu^{\prime}|
, as another tunneling amplitude, still depends exponentially on a function of

(\frac{H_{0}}{\delta},\frac{b}{a},\frac{H_{0}}{H_{1}})
.
Therefore, by the same procedure of restricting
|\nu|
,
|\nu^{\prime}|
can be set to be
o_{d}(g_{1})
.
Now, there is only one free variable left in
(\frac{H_{0}}{\delta},\frac{b}{a},\frac{H_{0}}{H_{1}})
.
Making use of the left one free variable, we can demand
(\frac{H_{0}}{\delta},\frac{b}{a},\frac{H_{0}}{H_{1}})
to converge to non-zero constants when
d\to\infty
.
In this case,
a
,
b
,
H_{0}
,
H_{1}
, and
\delta
all have limits and
previous constraints (independent of determining
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
) on these variables can be satisfied.
Since
\delta
has a non-zero limit, and
|\nu|=\Theta(1/\mathrm{poly}(d))
the condition
|\nu|\ll\mu=\Omega(\delta)
can be satisfied
for sufficiently large
d
.

In the double-well case, initiating QTW from the given local ground state
\left|\Phi_{-}\right\rangle
,
Lemma
3.1
can be simplified.
Note that the
O(h^{\infty})
term in
Lemma
3.1
is due to the integral

\int|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

which can be re-estimated as

\displaystyle\int|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq\Big(\int|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\Big)^{1/2}\Big(\int|\left\langle{\Phi_{+}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\Big)^{1/2}=1.

(437)

Thus, for the present case, we can write
T_{\rm mix}=O(1/\epsilon|\nu|)=O(\mathrm{poly}(d)/\epsilon)
(note that
\Delta E=2|\nu|
here).
Under
T_{\rm mix}=O(1/(\epsilon/2)|\nu|)=O(1/\epsilon|\nu|)
, we have

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\rm QTW}(T_{\rm mix},\cdot)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(\cdot)\|_{1}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}.

(438)

Let
\Phi(t)=e^{-iHt}\Phi(0)
with the initial state
\Phi(0)
.
By quantum simulation, we cannot get exactly the state
\Phi(t)

but an approximation
\tilde{\Phi}(t)
.
Set
\tilde{\rho}_{\rm QTW}(T_{\rm mix},x)=\int_{0}^{T_{\rm mix}}|\left\langle x\right|\tilde{\Phi}(t)\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}t/T_{\rm mix}
,
the condition

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\Phi}(t)-\Phi(t)\|\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2}\penalty\ (t\leq T_{\rm mix})

(439)

gives

\displaystyle\|\rho_{\rm QTW}(T_{\rm mix},\cdot)-\tilde{\rho}_{\rm QTW}(T_{\rm mix},\cdot)\|_{1}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{2},

(440)

which requires the number of quantum queries to be

\displaystyle O\left(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}t\frac{\log(2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}t/\epsilon)}{\log\log(2\|f\|_{L^{\infty}}t/\epsilon)}\right)=O\left(H_{2}t\frac{\log(2H_{2}t/\epsilon)}{\log\log(2H_{2}t/\epsilon)}\right)

(441)

(see
Lemma
B.2
in
Appendix
B.1
).
After simulating for some time bounded by
T_{\rm mix}
, measurements are equivalent to sampling from
\tilde{\rho}_{\rm QTW}(T_{\rm mix},\cdot)
,
and we have

\displaystyle\|\tilde{\rho}_{\rm QTW}(T_{\rm mix},\cdot)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(\cdot)\|_{1}\leq\epsilon.

(442)

Next, we need to analyze
\mu_{\rm QTW}
to determine the number of iterations and then the number of total queries.
In our case, the probability of finding the particle at
W_{+}
is given by

\displaystyle\int_{W_{+}}\mu_{\rm QTW}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle=\int_{W_{+}}[\frac{1}{2}(|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}+|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{-}}\right\rangle|^{2})+2\mathrm{Re}(\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi(0)}\right\rangle\left\langle{\Phi(0)}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle)\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle]\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

(443)

\displaystyle\geq\frac{1}{2}\int_{W_{+}}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\int_{W_{+}}|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

(444)

For
\mathbf{x}\in W_{+}
, we have
\mathbf{x}-2(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{v})\mathbf{v}+2\mathbf{b}\in W_{-}
, and thus the following holds because of the symmetry between
\Phi_{+}
and
\Phi_{-}
:

\displaystyle\frac{|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|}{|\left\langle{\Phi_{+}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|}=\frac{|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|}{|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}-2(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{v})\mathbf{v}+2\mathbf{b}}\right\rangle|},\penalty\ \mathrm{for}\penalty\ \mathbf{x}\in W_{+}.

(445)

Under previously determined parameters,
\Phi_{-}
and
\Phi_{+}
concentrate near
W_{-}
and
W_{+}
respectively, giving
\frac{|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|}{|\left\langle{\Phi_{+}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|}\leq 1
for any
\mathbf{x}\in W_{+}
. Manipulating the parameters, for example, changing
S_{0}/h
to
S_{0}/h+C
for some constant
C>0
independent of
d
,
1/\nu
will be a larger polynomial and the ratio
\frac{|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|}{|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}-2(\mathbf{x}^{\top}\mathbf{v})\mathbf{v}+2\mathbf{b}}\right\rangle|}\penalty\ (\mathbf{x}\in W_{+})
at least reduces by constant times. So, it can always be done in making
\frac{|\left\langle{\Phi_{-}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|}{|\left\langle{\Phi_{+}}\right|\left.\!{\mathbf{x}}\right\rangle|}\leq\frac{1}{4}\penalty\ (\mathbf{x}\in W_{+})
while maintaining
1/\nu
as a polynomial in
d
.
And we have

\displaystyle\int_{W_{+}}\mu_{\rm QTW}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\geq\frac{1}{4}\int_{W_{+}}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}.

(446)

Next, we should estimate
\int_{W_{+}}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}
.
Note that

\displaystyle 1=\int|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}=(\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash(W_{-}\cup B_{\mathbf{v}}\cup W_{+})}+\int_{W_{-}}+\int_{B_{\mathbf{v}}}+\int_{W_{+}})|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

(447)

we estimate the integrals separately.
The potential energy of
\left|\Phi_{+}\right\rangle
should be of
O(H_{0})
, so that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash(W_{-}\cup B_{\mathbf{v}}\cup W_{+})}H_{2}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}=O(H_{0}),

(448)

giving that

\displaystyle\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d}\backslash(W_{-}\cup B_{\mathbf{v}}\cup W_{+})}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}=O(H_{0}/H_{2}).

(449)

So, we know
(\int_{W_{-}}+\int_{B_{\mathbf{v}}}+\int_{W_{+}})|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}
is larger than some constant
C_{1}
independent of
d
.
Because
\left|\Phi_{+}\right\rangle
is the local ground state corresponding to
U_{+}
, it concentrates in
U_{+}
and promises that
\int_{W_{+}}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\geq\int_{W_{-}}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}
.
Note that the volume of
B_{\mathbf{v}}
is exponentially smaller than that of
W_{+}
, that is,

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{Volume(B_{\mathbf{v}})}}{\mathrm{Volume}(W_{+})}\leq\frac{(\sqrt{3}a/2)^{d-1}\mathrm{Area}(\mathbb{S}^{d-1})(2b-a)}{a^{d}\mathrm{Volume}(\mathbb{S}^{d})}=O(\mathrm{poly}(e^{-d})).

(450)

After steps of determining
\nu
and
\nu^{\prime}
, we still have a free variable, say,
H_{0}/H_{1}
.
We set
H_{0}/H_{1}=1
,
and the energy of a particle restricted in
B_{\mathbf{v}}
will significantly larger than that restricted in
W_{+}
.
Therefore, for the local ground state
\left|\Phi_{+}\right\rangle
, we can also have
\int_{W_{+}}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\geq\int_{B_{\mathbf{v}}}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}
.
In all,

\displaystyle C_{1}\leq\Big(\int_{W_{-}}+\int_{B_{\mathbf{v}}}+\int_{W_{+}}\Big)|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\leq 3\int_{W_{+}}|\left\langle{\mathbf{x}}\right|\left.\!{\Phi_{+}}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x},

(451)

and then

\displaystyle\int_{W_{+}}\mu_{\rm QTW}\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}\geq C_{2},

(452)

where
C_{2}:=C_{1}/12
is a constant independent of
d
.
Choose
\epsilon=C_{2}/2
to be a constant independent of
d
,
we have

\displaystyle\int_{W_{+}}\tilde{\rho}_{\rm QTW}(T_{\rm mix},\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

\displaystyle\geq\int_{W_{+}}\mu_{\rm QTW}(\mathbf{x})\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}-\int_{W_{+}}|\tilde{\rho}_{\rm QTW}(T_{\rm mix},\mathbf{x})-\mu_{\rm QTW}(\mathbf{x})|\mathrm{d}\mathbf{x}

(453)

\displaystyle\geq C_{2}-\|\tilde{\rho}_{\rm QTW}(T_{\rm mix},\cdot)-\mu_{\rm QTW}(\cdot)\|_{1}\geq C_{2}/2.

(454)

Let
C_{3}:=C_{2}/2
be also a constant independent of

d
.
After evolving the system for at most
T_{\rm mix}
once
the probability of hitting
W_{+}
is at least
C_{3}
.
Then, using
n
interactions which takes a total time bounded by
T_{\rm tot}=nT_{\rm mix}=nO(\mathrm{poly}(d))
,

19
19

19

Here,
\epsilon
has been set to be a constant and can be omitted.
the probability of successfully hitting
W_{+}
is
p_{\rm suc}\geq 1-(1-C_{3})^{n}
.
20
20

20

In the proof, we have determined
H_{2}
,
h
,
H_{1}
,
H_{0}
,
a
and
b
; the only remained parameter is

. We demand

to be a constant independent of
d
. In this case, as long as
h=4\delta/\sqrt{2}\omega d
, the Gaussian integral used in
Proposition
A.6
which gives
Lemma
4.16
is accurate enough for large
d
, and our conclusion based on
Lemma
4.16
is not affected.

Because of (
441
) and note that
H_{2}
and

are constants independent of

,
the number of total quantum quires is

. Since

.
∎

D.2.8
Proof of
Proposition
4.3

Let
\mathbf{n}
be a unit vector, we define the cone
\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathbf{n})
as

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathbf{n}):=\{\mathbf{x}:\mathbf{x}\cdot\mathbf{n}/\|\mathbf{x}\|>q\}.

(455)

For
f
defined by (
4.3
), the regions

B_{\mathbf{v}}
and
W_{+}
can be in a cone
\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathbf{v})
for some constant
q>\sqrt{3}/2
.

Suppose there are
N
directions
\mathbf{n}_{j}\penalty\ (j=1,...,N)
such that all
\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathbf{n}_{j})\penalty\ (j=1,...,N)
are disjoint.
We first show that an unstructured search with datasize
N
can be solved by solving
Problem
4.1
.
Let the datapoints be labeled by
\alpha\in\{1,...,N\}
.
In the unstructured search problem, we are given an oracle
\mathcal{O}
giving
\mathcal{O}(\alpha)
the value assigned to
\alpha
. Recall that there are only one point is assigned
1
and others are assigned
0
. Our goal is to find the only point, say
\alpha_{s}
, such that
\mathcal{O}(\alpha_{s})=1
.
Every point in unstructured search,
\alpha
, can be made uniquely correspond to a cone
\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathbf{n}_{\alpha})
.

Let
f_{0}
be a function satisfying (
4.3
) whose
\mathbf{v}:=\mathbf{n}_{0}
. Using the oracle
\mathcal{O}
and
f_{0}
, we can construct a new function
f
. If
\|\mathbf{x}\|\leq a
,
f(\mathbf{x})=f_{0}(\mathbf{x})
, For any
\mathbf{x}>a
, first determine whether
\mathbf{x}\in\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathbf{n}_{j})
for some
j
. If so,
f(\mathbf{x})=f_{0}(R(j,0)\mathbf{x})
for
\mathcal{O}(j)=1
and
f(\mathbf{x})=H_{2}
for
\mathcal{O}(j)=0
, where
R(j,0)
is a rotation mapping
\mathbf{n}_{j}
to
\mathbf{n}_{0}
. Otherwise,
f(\mathbf{x})=H_{2}
.
From the above construction, we know that
f
satisfies the following properties:

•

This
f
is a function of the form (
4.3
) whose
\mathbf{v}=\mathbf{n}_{\alpha_{s}}
.

•

One query to the function value of
f
need at most one query to the oracle
\mathcal{O}
.

Therefore, if we can solve
Problem
4.1
on this
f
within queries (to
f
) polynomial in
d
, we can have an algorithm for the
N
-size unstructured search finding
\alpha_{s}
within queries (to
\mathcal{O}
) polynomial in
d
. With the fact
N
is exponential in
d
, assuming
Problem
4.1
can be solved within queries polynomial in
d
will violate the quantum lower bound for unstructured search, i.e.,
\Omega(\sqrt{N})

Bennett et al. 1997
.

Next, to prove
Proposition
4.3
by contradiction, we just need to show that
N
can be exponential in
d
.
The only constrain on
N
is that
\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathbf{n}_{j})\cap\mathcal{C}_{q}(\mathbf{n}_{k})=\varnothing
for
j\neq k
, which is equivalent to
\|\mathbf{n}_{j}-\mathbf{n}_{k}\|\geq\varepsilon
for
j\neq k
and some
\varepsilon<1
.
The following Lemma provides a desired estimation of
N
.

Lemma D.1
.

There exists a set of
N
unit vectors in
\mathbb{R}^{d}
(or
N
points on
\mathbb{S}^{d-1}
) such that

•

\forall\mathbf{x}\neq\mathbf{y}
in the set,
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|\geq\varepsilon
;

•

N\geq(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}+\frac{1}{2})^{d}-(\frac{1}{2\varepsilon}-\frac{1}{2})^{d}
.

Proof.

Let the set of points be
A
, we consider an argument that as long as there is a point
\mathbf{x}
satisfying
\min_{\mathbf{y}\in A}\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{y}\|\geq\varepsilon
, we add
\mathbf{x}
to
A
.

If we cannot add any more point to
A
, the
N
is possibly the largest. In this case, consider any point
\mathbf{z}
with
1-\varepsilon\leq\|\mathbf{z}\|\leq 1+\varepsilon
, there must exists
\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{z}}\in A
such that
\|\mathbf{x}_{\mathbf{z}}-\mathbf{z}/\|\mathbf{z}\|\|<\varepsilon
.
Note that
\|\mathbf{z}-\mathbf{z}/\|\mathbf{z}\|\|<\varepsilon
,
by the triangle inequality, we obtain
\|\mathbf{x}-\mathbf{z}\|\leq 2\varepsilon
.
This means that the balls
\{\mathbb{B}(\mathbf{x},2\varepsilon)\mid\mathbf{x}\in A\}
cover the region
\{\mathbf{z}:1-\varepsilon\leq\|\mathbf{z}\|\leq 1+\varepsilon\}
, giving

\displaystyle N(2\varepsilon)^{d}\geq(1+\varepsilon)^{d}-(1-\varepsilon)^{d},

(456)

which completes the proof.
∎

D.3
Mollified functions

For any
r>0
, let
m_{r}\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
to be the mollifier function of width
r
, which is given by

\displaystyle m_{r}(x)=\left\{\begin{array}[]{l}\frac{1}{I_{d}}\exp\left(-\frac{1}{1-\|x/r\|^{2}}\right),\penalty\ x\in\mathbb{B}(0,r)\\
0,\penalty\ \mathrm{otherwise},\end{array}\right.

where
I_{d}
is the normalize constant such that
\int_{\mathbb{B}(0,r)}m_{r}(x)\mathrm{d}x=1
.
The mollification of
f
by
m_{r}(x)
is defined as

\displaystyle F_{r}(x):=(f*m_{r})(x)=\int f(x-y)m_{r}(y)\mathrm{d}x.

(459)

The mollified functions satisfy the following desired property:

Lemma D.2
.

Let
f\colon\mathbb{R}^{d}\to\mathbb{R}
and
F_{r}=f*m_{r}
, then
F_{r}
is infinitely differentiable and
F_{r}\to f_{r}\penalty\ (r\to 0)
.

Lemma
D.2
follows from the properties of convolution and the fact
m_{r}
is infinitely differentiable.

D.4
Distribution of wells

Here we show that in high dimensions, the distribution of wells
will affect coefficient of the exponential term
e^{S_{0}/h}
in the
evolution time of QTW.
We assume that tunneling amplitude
w
is the same and all local ground
states have the energy
\mu
. Thus, the only variable is the “distribution".

Let us see the example that the wells form a ring. The interaction matrix can be written as

H_{|\mathcal{F}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccccc}\mu&w&&&&w\\
w&\mu&w&&&\\
&w&\mu&w&&\\
&&\ddots&\ddots&\ddots&\\
&&&w&\mu&w\\
w&&&&w&\mu\\
\end{array}\right).

(460)

We have

E_{k}=2|w|\cos\frac{k\pi}{N+1}+\mu,\penalty\ k=1,2,\ldots,N.

(461)

Therefore,

\sum_{E_{k}=E_{k^{\prime}}}\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|i\rangle\langle i|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle=\sum_{k}|\langle E_{k}|i\rangle|^{2}|\langle E_{k}|j\rangle|^{2}\geq\frac{1}{2N}.

(462)

The last equality is secured by the fact that

\langle E_{k}|j\rangle\sim\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}e^{-\frac{ikj\pi}{N+1}}.

(463)

The energy gap can be calculated as

\Delta E=2|w|\left(\cos\frac{\pi}{N+1}-\cos\frac{2\pi}{N+1}\right)=\Omega\left(\frac{1}{N^{2}}\right)|w|.

(464)

If we set
\tau>\frac{N}{\epsilon\Delta E}
for a small constant
\epsilon>0
, then

p(\tau,j|i)\geq\frac{1}{2N}\left(1-\frac{3N}{\tau\Delta E}\right)\geq\frac{1}{2N}\left(1-3\epsilon\right).

(465)

And the time for one trial can be

\frac{N}{\epsilon\Delta E}=O\left(\frac{N^{3}}{\epsilon}\right)\frac{1}{|w|}.

(466)

Repeating trials for
1/p(\tau,j|i)
times, we can hit the
j
th well with probability
\Omega(1)
, and the total time can be bounded by

\frac{N}{\epsilon\Delta Ep(\tau,j|i)}=O\left(\frac{N^{4}}{\epsilon}\right)\frac{1}{|w|}.

(467)

Consider the most symmetric case, where the interaction matrix is given by

H_{|\mathcal{F}}=\left(\begin{array}[]{cccc}\mu&w&\cdots&w\\
w&\mu&w&\vdots\\
\vdots&w&\ddots&w\\
w&\cdots&w&\mu\\
\end{array}\right).

(468)

The eigenvalues are

E_{1}=\cdots=E_{N-1}=\mu-w,\penalty\ E_{N}=\mu+(N-1)w,

(469)

giving the energy gap
\Delta E=N|w|
.
The corresponding eigenstates can be given by

|E_{k}\rangle=\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\sum_{j}e^{i\frac{2\pi}{N}kj}|j\rangle,

(470)

which gives

\sum_{E_{k}=E_{k^{\prime}}}\langle j|E_{k}\rangle\langle E_{k}|i\rangle\langle i|E_{k^{\prime}}\rangle\langle E_{k^{\prime}}|j\rangle=\left\{\begin{array}[]{c}\frac{2}{N^{2}},\penalty\ i\neq j,\\
1-\frac{2(N-1)}{N^{2}},\penalty\ i=j,\end{array}\right.

(471)

and

\sum_{k}|\langle E_{k}|i\rangle|^{2}|\langle E_{k}|j\rangle|^{2}=\frac{1}{N}.

(472)

For
i\neq j
, we will have that

p(\tau,j|i)\geq\frac{2}{N^{2}}-\frac{4}{\tau\Delta E}\frac{N-1}{N^{2}}\geq\frac{2}{N^{2}}\left(1-2\epsilon\right),

(473)

if
\tau>\frac{N}{\epsilon\Delta E}
where
\epsilon
is a small constant.
The time needed for one trial can be bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{N}{\epsilon\Delta E}=O(1/\epsilon)\frac{1}{|w|},

(474)

which is independent of
N
.
Repeating
1/p(\tau,j|i)
times, we are able to hit the
j
th well with high probability and the total time is bounded by

\displaystyle\frac{N}{\epsilon\Delta Ep(\tau,j|i)}=O(N^{2}/\epsilon)\frac{1}{|w|}.

(475)

Comparing (
475
) with (
467
), the distribution of well
can reduce the coefficient before
1/|w|
from
O(N^{4}/\epsilon)
to
O(N^{2}/\epsilon)
,
which is meaningful especially when
N
is large.

Appendix E
Full Numerical Experiments

In this appendix, we present detailed numerical results testing our theoretical analysis and demonstrating our quantum speedup claims.
All experiments are performed on
a classical computer (with Dual-Core Intel
Core i5 Processor, 16GB memory) using MATLAB 2020b.

In
Appendix
E.1
, we run QTW and SGD on concrete examples
provided by
Section
4.2
. Initial state preparation is numerically explored and discussed. Subsequently, QTW is implemented
and compared with SGD, demonstrating the power of quantum tunneling
as expected by our theory.
In
Appendix
E.2
,
classical lower bound proved in
Section
4.3
(i.e.,
Proposition
4.1
) is tested for a specific classical algorithm, SGD.
At last, the dependence of QTW running time on quantum learning rate

h
is highlighted by a experiment in
Appendix
E.3
,
supporting our result,
Theorem
1.1
.
For experiments involving QTW,
we only deal with low-dimensional landscapes due to the limitation of solving the Schrödinger equation by classical numerical methods.

For numerical integration of the Schrödinger equation,
we follow
Zhang et al. 2021a
and
Gray and Manolopoulos 1996
. We first discretize the space such that the Schrödinger equation is approximated by a linear system (see
Appendix
B.1
for details of discretization),

\displaystyle i\frac{\mathrm{d}\Phi}{\mathrm{d}t}=\hat{H}\Phi,

(476)

where
\hat{H}
is a matrix approximating the Hamiltonian and
\Phi
is a complex valued vector simulating the wave function.
Then, we write
\Phi(t)=Q(t)+iP(t)
for
Q
and
P
being the real and imaginary part of
\Phi
, respectively.
The discretized Schrödinger equation is equivalent to
a separable Hamiltonian system:

\displaystyle\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}Q=\hat{H}P,\quad\frac{\mathrm{d}}{\mathrm{d}t}P=-\hat{H}Q.

(477)

In practice, we use a symplectic leap frog scheme to solve
this Hamiltonian system
François 2020
. This also has connection to recent literature on symplectic optimization
Betancourt et al. 2018
;
Jordan 2018
.

SGD refers to the iterative algorithm
x_{k+1}=x_{k}-s\nabla f(x_{k})-s\xi_{k}
, where
f
is the objective function and
\xi_{k}
is the noise of the
k
th step.
Queries to the gradients of
f
are permitted and
\xi_{k}

is normally distributed with unit variance.

E.1
Tests on quantum-classical comparisons

Our first set of experiments are conducted on one-dimensional
examples in
Section
4.2
: the critical case (
Example
4.1
),
the case stressing flatness of minima (
Example
4.2
), and the case
stressing sharpness of barriers (
Example
4.3
).
Parameters of the landscapes used in
Appendix
E.1
are defined in
Section
4.2
.

To make the experiments non-trivial and
still can be efficiently executed on our computer,
we choose the number of minima
N=3
.
In solving the discretized Schrödinger equation, we fix the spatial domain to be
\Omega=[0,6a+4b]
and the mesh number to be 512, where
a
,
3a+2b
,
5a+4b
are the three minima.
The problem to be solved is specified as hitting
[4a+4b,6a+4b]
beginning at one minimum
a
.

With the knowledge of one minimum
a
,
we approximate the local ground state corresponding to
a
.
Let
\Omega_{1}
be a neighborhood of
a
, the Hamiltonian restricted in the domain
\Omega_{1}
is also discretized as a matrix
\hat{H}_{\Omega_{1}}
.
We solve the eigenvectors of
\hat{H}_{\Omega_{1}}
and
\hat{H}
in MATLAB.
By our theory, the ideal initial state
\left|\Phi(0)\right\rangle
prepared should be in
the low energy subspace
\mathcal{F}
spanned by the three eigenstates of
\hat{H}
with smallest eigenvalues, i.e.,
\left|E_{j}\right\rangle,\penalty\ j=1,2,3
.
Whether the ground state of
\hat{H}_{\Omega_{1}}
is nearly in

\mathcal{F}
depends on the quantum learning rate
h
and the region
\Omega_{1}
.
We numerically discuss error estimation of initial state preparation (local ground state preparation)
here as a supplement to
Appendix
B.2
.
To this end, we prepare the ground state of
\hat{H}_{\Omega_{1}}

for small local region (i.e.,
\Omega_{1}=[0,2a]
), middle local region (i.e.,
\Omega_{1}=[0,2a+b]
), and large local region (i.e.,
\Omega_{1}=[0,2a+2b]
) under various
h
. The probability or overlap of
\left|\Phi(0)\right\rangle
in
\mathcal{F}
given by

\displaystyle|\left\langle{\Phi(0)}\right|\left.\!{E_{1}}\right\rangle|^{2}+|\left\langle{\Phi(0)}\right|\left.\!{E_{2}}\right\rangle|^{2}+|\left\langle{\Phi(0)}\right|\left.\!{E_{3}}\right\rangle|^{2}

(478)

is numerically calculated whose results are shown in
Figure
13
.

Figure 13:
Overlap between the local ground state and the low energy subspace
\mathcal{F}
: Example 1, 2, and 3 correspond to
Example
4.1
,
Example
4.2
, and
Example
4.3
, respectively.

For all examples, the larger
h
is, the more unlikely the local state prepared
\Phi(0)
is to be in
\mathcal{F}
.
For
Example
4.1
, small, middle, and large local regions have similar results as
b\ll a
in
Example
4.1
.
Comparing
Example
4.1
with
Example
4.2
,
a thicker barrier (a larger
b
) yields lager differences
between small, middle, and large local regions.
However, if the barrier is high, as shown by
Example
4.3
,
results for the three local regions can be close to each other again.
This may be accounted for by that wave functions decay rapidly in the barrier region,
[2a,2a+2b]
, and the local ground state concentrate in
[0,2a]
regardless of whether
\Omega_{1}=[0,2a]
or
[0,2a+b]
or
[a,2a+2b]
.

Due to the large running time cost of solving the Schrödinger equation on classical computers,
21
21

21

In this section, we use
running time
to refer to the actual time spent by our classical computer to solve the problem, and we use
evolution time
to denote the time variable in simulating QTW and SGD.
we
simulate QTW for each example choosing one
h
and one initial state.
In practice, preparing the initial state in a smaller region may be
more convenient while producing a larger error.
We choose the small local region for each example to prepare the initial state, and the chosen
h
for different examples are shown in
Table
1
.
On the one hand, we need
h
to be small to obtain an initial state largely staying in
\mathcal{F}
.
On the other hand,
h
should not be too small, such that the running time is tractable on classical computers. As shown in
Table
1
, all chosen
h
manage to make at least 90% of the initial state
be in
\mathcal{F}
.

Choices of

for different examples. Overlap denotes the overlap of the initial state prepared

\mathcal{F}
.

Having obtained
h
and the prepared initial state
\Phi(0)
for each
example, we need to determine the time
\tau
for QTW.
Recall that the evolution time of one QTW trial,
t
, is uniformly chosen from

[0,\tau]
(see
Section
3.2
), and we repeat the trials until success.
As long as
\tau
is large enough, the probability of success in one trial will be larger than some constant (see
Section
3.3
and
Section
3.4
).
Practically, we determine
\tau
by testing a geometric series (this is a common trick in randomized algorithms), i.e.,
\tau=1,2,4,8,\ldots
and repeat the experiments for many times. If the success probability is too small, we just double
\tau
until we reach a value where the success probability is decent.
For simplicity, we discuss and determine
\tau
here by directly observe
the evolution of
\Phi(t)
.

Figure
14
illustrates
a typical quantum tunneling process on the landscape
Example
4.1
.
The time
288
when the wave function concentrates in the target well
[4a+ab,6a+4b]
is defined as the characteristic time
T^{\star}_{1}
for
Example
4.1
.
Similarly, for
Example
4.2
and
Example
4.3
, we can find
the characteristic time
T^{\star}_{2}=800
and
T^{\star}_{3}=600
, respectively.

Figure 14:
Evolution of the wave function on
Example
4.1
:
the blue line represents the probability distributing
|\left\langle{\Phi(t)}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|^{2}

and the red line is the landscape
f
.

Setting
\tau
as the half characteristic time, the characteristic time, and the double characteristic time, respectively, we repeat our experiments and obtain the average hitting time.
Rigorously, we use an
experiment
to denote a process repeating trials until
successfully hitting
[4a+ab,6a+4b]
.
A
trial
initiates the simulation at
\Phi(0)
and
measures the position at
t
randomly chosen from
[0,\tau]
.
The average hitting time means the
average total evolution time of different experiments. Results of average hitting time are presented in
Table
2
, each of which is obtained from 20 experiments.

Figure 15:

T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}
and the SGD expected risk on the learning rate
s
. Each experiment is repeated for 1000 times, the (blue) circles denote the average hitting time, and one error bar represents the range of the corresponding 1000 data.

and each example.

Theoretically, when
\tau
is large enough, the probability of successfully
hitting in one trial is near a constant
p_{\rm suc}
, so that the number of trials needed in one experiment is also approximately a constant
1/p_{\rm suc}
.
The expected time for each trial is
\tau/2
. Thus, for large enough
\tau
,
the expected time of one experiment is approximately
\tau/p_{\rm suc}\propto\tau
.
Based on results of
Table
2
, the characteristic time
T^{\star}

is large enough, and is chosen as
\tau
in later experiments.

Having specified the values of
h
and
\tau
,
we conduct 1000 experiments for each example and study the distribution of hitting time.
The statistics of the results are shown in
Figure
8
.

Next, we proceed by studying the hitting time of SGD.
Taking fair comparisons into consideration, we employ
Standard
4.1
in

Section
4.1
which equalizes the expected risks yielded by SGD and QTW.
The expected risk of QTW is numerically determined by
calculating

\displaystyle\frac{1}{T}\int_{0}^{T}\Big(\int_{\Omega}f(x)|\left\langle{\Phi(t)}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}x\Big)\mathrm{d}t

(479)

for
T=1000
.
The expected risk st
t
,
\int_{\Omega}f(x)|\left\langle{\Phi(t)}\right|\left.\!{x}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}x
,
oscillates rapidly with respect to time but converges to a fixed value; see for instance
Figure
19
for a one-dimensional case. Thus, we pick a long enough time
T
to calculate an expected risk for all time.
For
Example
4.1
,
Example
4.2
, and
Example
4.3
, the expected risks given by (
479
) are
0.0324\pm 0.0054
,
0.0222\pm 0.0051
, and
0.0377\pm 0.0070
, respectively.
Using the Gibbs distribution (
19
)
\mu_{\rm SGD}
for the calculation of
the expected risk

\displaystyle\int\mu_{\rm SGD}(x)f(x)\mathrm{d}x,

(480)

we can plot the relationship between the expected risk and the learning rate
s

as shown in
Figure
15
.

For each example, we calculate the hitting time for various
s
, which is also plotted in
Figure
15
.
Theoretically,
\ln T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}\sim 2H_{f}/s
with
2H_{f}
being the Morse saddle barrier of
f
, which agrees well with the results in
Figure
15
.

To make the expected risks of QTW and SGD equal,
we set learning rates
s=0.1525
,
s=0.129
, and
s=0.189
for

Example
4.1
,
Example
4.2
, and
Example
4.3
, respectively.
For each example and corresponding learning rate
s
, we
run SGD for 1000 times and draw the histogram of SGD hitting time in

Figure
8
.
We use
T^{\rm QTW}_{\rm hit}
and
T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}

to denote the evolution time of one experiment for QTW and SGD, respectively.
In
Figure
8
, the histograms compare
T^{\rm QTW}_{\rm hit}
with
T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}/10
, and
all presented examples demonstrate that QTW is faster.
The number of quantum queries is approximately

\tilde{O}(\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{\Omega}}T^{\rm QTW}_{\rm hit})
and the number of classical queries is
\Omega(T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}/s)
.
In addition, in the three examples
\|f\|_{L^{\infty}_{\Omega}}\leq 0.85
and
s<0.2
, and
quantum advantage exists in terms of query complexity.

This result matches our theory at large.
For
Example
4.1
, we make direct comparison between the exponential terms
e^{S_{0}/h}
and
e^{2H_{f}/s}
,and to remove the coefficients in front of them,
we divide
T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}
by
10
such that
T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}/10
has similar distribution t
T^{\rm QTW}_{\rm hit}
for
Example
4.1
.
In this way, we observe that whether
T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}/10
is relatively larger than
T^{\rm QTW}_{\rm hit}
is determined only by
e^{S_{0}/h}
and
e^{2H_{f}/s}
.

For
Example
4.2
,
T^{\rm QTW}_{\rm hit}
is not much smaller than
T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}/10
, which is not completely coherent with our theory.
This result can be explained as that for
Example
4.2
, the quantum learning rate
h
is not small enough such that the initial state prepared does not well stay near a low energy subspace.
Specifically,
Table
1
shows that initial states of both
Example
4.1
and
Example
4.3
are largely
in
\mathcal{F}
(more than 95%), while about 90% of the initial state of
Example
4.2
overlaps with

\mathcal{F}
.
Experiments on
Example
4.2
suggest that higher energy may not be able to help quantum tunneling to run faster.

For
Example
4.3
, significant quantum speedup is achieved as expected. As shown by
Figure
8
,
T^{\rm SGD}_{\rm hit}/10
is even several orders of magnitude larger than
T^{\rm QTW}_{\rm hit}
.

E.2
Dimension dependence

Figure 16:
Distribution of the number of steps
Q_{\rm esc}
needed to escape
S_{\mathbf{v}}
: for each
s
and each
d
, 1000 samples are gathered.

Here we examine part of
Section
4.3
by testing SGD and the classical lower bound.

The classical lower bound in
Section
4.3
ensures that
for any
s
, SGD cannot cannot escape from
S_{\mathbf{v}}
with subexponential queries with high probability.
Based on the constructed landscape (
4.3
) with parameters specified by
a=1
,
R=4\sqrt{2}a
,
w=a/2
,
\omega=0.5
,
b=1.4
,
H_{1}=H_{0}
, and
H_{2}=20H_{0}
, we test SGD with different learning rates (
s\in[0.1,1]
)
in various dimensions (
d\in[15,95]
).
For each dimension and each
s
, 1000 experiments are conducted.
The number of steps spent to escaping from
S_{\mathbf{v}}
in one experiment is denoted as
Q_{\rm esc}
.

The distributions of
Q_{\rm esc}
under different dimensions for
s=1
, 0.5, and 0.25 are shown in
Figure
16
.
With the increase of
d
, all characterized values of the distribution gradually grows exponentially in terms of
d
, supporting our theory.

We also present the relationship between the average
Q_{\rm esc}
and the dimension
d
in
Figure
10
.
A counterintuitive fact is that for a fixed dimension
d
, SGD with larger
s
is more difficult to escape
S_{\mathbf{v}}
, which needs further explanation.
For each fixed learning rate
s
, we observe that with the increase of
d
, the average
Q_{\rm esc}
remains constant initially and then increase exponentially with respect to
d
.
Increasing
s
yields a smaller initial constant but larger exponential rate.
Nevertheless, for all
s
,
Q_{\rm esc}
eventually increases
exponentially with respect to
d
.
With the prediction in
Section
4.3
, if the number of queries is of the order
e^{\frac{dw^{2}}{2R^{2}}}=e^{d/256}
, the probability of escaping
S_{\mathbf{v}}
will no longer exponentially small.
The lower bound of average
Q_{\rm esc}
increases larger than
e^{d/256}
, supporting the prediction.

E.3
Quantum tunneling and the quantum learning rate
h

In QTW, the quantum learning rate
h
is one of the most important variables.

Theorem
1.1
gives a general relationship between
h
and the evolution time of QTW.
We further test the relationship on the landscape constructed in
Section
4.3
(dimension
d=2
) with specified parameters given in the same as
Appendix
E.2
:
a=1
,
R=4\sqrt{2}a
,
w=a/2
,
\omega=0.5
,
b=1.4
,
H_{1}=H_{0}
, and
H_{2}=20H_{0}
. The Schrödinger equation is solved in the region
\Omega=\{(x,y):|x|<4,|y|<4\}

and the mesh number is 399 on each edge.

Since the landscape has two symmetric wells, the time for tunneling from one well to the other,
T_{\rm half}
, is explicitly linked to
\Delta E
, i.e.,
T_{\rm half}=\pi/\Delta E
.
On this concrete landscape,
\Delta E
can be predicted using
Lemma
4.16
and note that
\Delta E=2|\nu|
, we have

\displaystyle\ln T_{\rm half}=\frac{S_{0}}{h}-\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{2h}{\pi}+\ln\frac{\pi}{2}-\frac{1}{2}\ln\frac{2H_{1}\omega^{2}}{4\sqrt{H_{1}}/b}-\frac{2\omega(b-a)}{\sqrt{2H_{1}}}+4\ln\frac{b}{a}.

(481)

The initial state is prepared in the small region
\Omega_{1}=\{(x,y):|x|<2,|y|<2\}
. Varying
h
, the overlap between the initial state and the low-energy subspace
\mathcal{F}
is shown in
Figure
17
.
We choose
h\in[0.16,0.28]
such that the overlap is approximately in
[0.85,0.95]
.

Figure 17:
The relationship between the overlap and
h
.

Starting from one well, we stop when the probability of finding
the other well exceeds 90% and record the evolution time as
T_{\rm half}
. The probability of finding
the other well is numerically calculated based on

\displaystyle\int_{W_{+}}|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{\Phi(t)}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}x.

(482)

For
h=0.2
,
T_{\rm half}
is approximately 200, and we show the
evolution of the wave function in
Figure
18
.

Figure 18:
Wave function evolution for
h=0.2
. The upper part of all the nine figures shows the wave packet at different time and the lower part is the contour map of the landscape showing
W_{-}
,
B_{\mathbf{v}}
, and
W_{+}
.

The expect risk

\displaystyle\int_{\Omega}f(x)|\left\langle{x}\right|\left.\!{\Phi(t)}\right\rangle|^{2}\mathrm{d}x

(483)

with respect to time is plotted in
Figure
19
, which oscillates rapidly but maintains to be near 0.065.

Numerical results on
T_{\rm half}
are shown in
Figure
10
. The results match our theory
except a constant difference between the predicted and experimental
\ln T_{\rm half}
, indicating the correctness of
\ln T_{\rm half}=\frac{S_{0}}{h}-\frac{1}{2}\ln h+\cdots
.
The constant difference emerges because we stop evolution when the probability of tunneling exceeds 90%, while the theoretical
T_{\rm half}
takes the time when the probability is nearly 100%.
Anyway, in studying the dependence of
T_{\rm half}
on
h
, a constant coefficient is insignificant.

Figure 19:
Expected risk when
h=0.2
with total running time 200.
